## From Zahur ul-Hasan Sharib, Tazkirah Awliya-e Pak o Hind

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/tazkirah_awliya_pak_o_hind.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- **Saint.** **The three ranges are grossly unequal and the reader must see it.** pp. 1–109 treats him at chapter length — باب 7 runs pp. 48–52 (folios 44–48) to its break, with the chapter's closing pages at pp. 53–55 (folios 49–51, **provisionally attributed, flagged under the pp. 1–109 input's Doubts D4**), plus his appearances as arbitrator in باب 2 (pp. 35–37, folios 31–33), as father and pir at the head of باب 11 (pp. 74–76, folios 70–72), and as a named contemporary in باب 8 (pp. 59, 63–64; folios 55, 59–60). pp. 110–210 makes him **never the subject of a chapter**: he is named throughout باب 17 (pp. 113–114, folios 109–110) and at three points in باب 22 (pp. 133, 135; folios 129, 131). pp. 211–303 engages him **twice, both times incidentally, and the two mentions do not overlap** (p. 222, folio 218; p. 234, folio 230).
  - **Identification and placement cautions, carried from all three inputs.** In pp. 110–210 the town agrees with the archive row by the book's own nisba, "**ملتانی**", used at every mention (pp. 113–114, folios 109–110), and the Suhrawardi affiliation is carried there **only** in the family khirqa of Shaikh Shihab al-Din Suhrawardi (p. 114, folio 110); باب 22 adds an independent placement — Jalal al-Din Surkh Bukhari "ملتان آئے۔ حضرت بہاء الدین زکریا رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کے مرید ہوئے", came to **ملتان**, Multan, and became his murid there (p. 133, folio 129) — which the pp. 110–210 worker records as **that range's only sentence placing Baha al-Din Zakariya himself, and it places him at Multan** (p. 133, folio 129). In pp. 211–303 the nisba "**ملتانی**" is the only locational element attached to him at p. 234 (folio 230). **A second, distinct "بہاء الدین" occupies باب 41 of the same pp. 211–303 chunk (p. 214, folio 210) and must not be merged with him** (p. 214, folio 210).
  - **Titles as printed**: "پیر لاثانی", peerless pir; "مقبول بارگاہ رحمانی"; "برہان ملت"; "گنج عزلت"; "مخزن سخا", treasury of generosity; "معدن وفا"; "کان صفا"; "قطب الاولیاء"; "شیخ الاتقیاء"; "قدوۃ الاصفیاء"; and "اکابر اولیاء میں آپ کا شمار ہے", he is counted among the greatest of the auliya (p. 48, folio 44). In the khirqa dream the Prophet names him "شیخ بہاء الدین غوث العالم" (p. 51, folio 47).
  - **Silsila as the book gives it — a relationship, never an order name.** "آپ حضرت شیخ شہاب الدین عمر سہروردی رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کے مرید اور خلیفہ ہیں" — murid and khalifa of **شیخ شہاب الدین عمر سہروردی**, Shaikh Shihab al-Din Umar Suhrawardi (p. 48, folio 44); the bai'at was taken at **بغداد**, Baghdad (p. 50, folio 46). **The book nowhere uses the word "سہروردیہ" as an order name in باب 7** (pp. 48–52, folios 44–48); chunk 004 records **silsila: (not stated)** for pp. 74–76 (folios 70–72); the pp. 110–210 range records **silsila: (not stated)** (pp. 113–114, 133, 135; folios 109–110, 129, 131); and the pp. 211–303 range records **silsila: (not stated)** in both mentions (p. 222, folio 218; p. 234, folio 230), which its worker labels "silence, not conflict". The relationship stated agrees with the archive row's "Suhrawardi" without the book naming the order (p. 48, folio 44).
  - **UNRECONCILED alongside the above — a Qadiri threshold in the book's own printed verse.** The same chapter prints his famous **منقبت, manqabat**, in honour of Ghaus al-Azam Shaikh Abd al-Qadir Jilani (p. 52, folio 48), whose signature couplet is "سنگ درگاہ جیلانی بہاء الدین ملتانی / لقائے[OCR?] دین سلطانی محی الدین جیلانی" — "Baha al-Din Multani is a stone of the threshold of Jilani" (p. 53, folio 49). **The Suhrawardi bai'at-and-khilafat statement (p. 48, folio 44) and this Qadiri-threshold verse (p. 53, folio 49) stand side by side; no input reconciles them and this merge does not either.**
  - **UNRECONCILED — lineage given as four accounts, and then, uniquely, adjudicated.** "آپ قریشی' اسدی' ہاشمی تھے" — Qurashi, Asadi, Hashimi (p. 48, folio 44, حاشیہ 1). "یہ بھی کہا جاتا ہے" that he was of the progeny of "مہیار[OCR?] بن اسود بن مطلب بن اسد بن عبد العزیٰ[OCR?] بن قصی" (p. 48, folio 44, حاشیہ 2). Some accepted only "القرشی الاسدی" (p. 48, folio 44, حاشیہ 3). Some wrote, in Persian, "سلسلہ او باسد قریش کہ جد مادری حضرت امیر المومنین علی کرم اللہ وجہہ منتہی می شود", his lineage ends at Asad of Quraish, the maternal grandfather of Ali, with the book printing its own Urdu translation beneath it (p. 48, folio 44, حاشیہ 4). **Then the one adjudication in the whole pp. 1–109 range**: "آپ ہاشمی ہیں اور آپ کے ہاشمی ہونے میں کوئی شک و شبہ نہیں" — he is Hashimi and in his being Hashimi there is no doubt whatever (p. 48, folio 44, حاشیہ 5).
  - **The family's route to Multan.** His "جد امجد" **Maulana Shaikh Kamal al-Din Aliya Shah[OCR?]**, a dervish-natured elder and honoured member of the Quraish, "مکہ سے سکونت ترک کرکے خوارزم میں رہے' پھر خوارزم سے ملتان تشریف لائے اور وہاں مستقل سکونت اختیار کی" — left **مکہ**, Mecca, lived in **خوارزم**, Khwarazm, then came from Khwarazm to **ملتان**, Multan, and settled there permanently (p. 48, folio 44, حاشیہ 6), where many became his devotees (p. 49, folio 45). **The book's stated reason**: "ملتان اس زمانہ میں اسلامی علوم و فنون کا مرکز تھا" — Multan in that age was a centre of Islamic sciences and arts (p. 48, folio 44).
  - **Father**: **شیخ وجیہہ الدین**, Shaikh Wajih al-Din (p. 49, folio 45).
  - **UNRECONCILED — two maternal grandfathers printed side by side, neither preferred.** The mother is "بی بی فاطمہ", Bibi Fatima (p. 49, folio 45). (a) She is given first as the daughter of **مولانا حسام الدین ترمذی**, Maulana Husam al-Din Tirmizi, who came from his homeland **ترمذ**, Tirmiz, to the fort of "قلعہ کوٹ کڑور[OCR?]" when the **Tatar plundering** began (p. 49, folio 45, حاشیہ 7). (b) Then, "یہ بھی کہا جاتا ہے", as the daughter of **حضرت عیسیٰ**, Hazrat Isa, of the progeny of Ghaus al-Azam Shaikh Jilani, resident at "ہامہ[OCR?]", who married her to Shaikh Wajih al-Din when he visited there (p. 49, folio 45). **Both accounts end with the family at Kot Karor[OCR?]; the book does not choose, and the pp. 1–109 input directs that any entry using his descent must give both** (p. 49, folio 45).
  - **Birth as printed**: "آپ صبح کے وقت' جمعہ کے دن 27 رمضان 566ھ کو کوٹ کڑور میں پیدا ہوئے" — born in the morning, on a **جمعہ**, Friday, **27 رمضان 566ھ**, 27 Ramazan 566 AH, at **کوٹ کڑور**, Kot Karor (p. 49, folio 45). Name: بہاء الدین, Bahauddin (p. 49, folio 45). Kunya **ابو محمد**, Abu Muhammad, and "بعض نے آپ کی کنیت ابو البرکات بھی لکھی ہے", some also wrote **ابو البرکات**, Abu al-Barakat — **an unreconciled pair, the book preferring neither** (p. 49, folio 45, حاشیہ 8). The pp. 110–210 range records **birth: (not stated)** (pp. 113–114, 133, 135; folios 109–110, 129, 131).
  - **Born a wali.** "آپ مادر زاد ولی تھے" (p. 49, folio 45) — as an infant he would stop suckling to listen when his father recited the Quran, and while still at maktab said "جس وقت خداوند تعالیٰ نے الست بربکم فرمایا تھا' اس وقت سے لے کر اب تک کے واقعات مجھے یاد ہیں", I remember what has happened from the moment God said *Am I not your Lord?* until now (p. 49, folio 45, حاشیہ 9). **His father died when he was twelve** (p. 49, folio 45).
  - **Training, quantified.** Memorised the Quran "سات سال کی عمر میں سات قرات کے ساتھ", at **seven** with the **seven readings**; after his father's death went to **خراسان**, Khurasan, and spent **seven years** in the outward lessons (p. 50, folio 46, حاشیہ 10); then **بخارا**, Bukhara, where he completed his learning and "وہاں آپ 'بہاء الدین فرشتہ' کے نام سے مشہور ہوئے", became famous there as **Bahauddin Farishta**; then **مدینہ**, Medina, where he took hadith from **شیخ کمال الدین محمد یمنی**, of the great muhaddithin, and "اجازت نامہ بھی حاصل کیا", obtained an **اجازت نامہ, ijazat-nama**, as well, staying **five years** (p. 50, folio 46). He met dervishes in "خراسان' بخارا' مکہ' مدینہ اور بیت المقدس" — Khurasan, Bukhara, Mecca, Medina and Bait al-Muqaddas (p. 50, folio 46).
  - **Seventy days that caused resentment, the numeral OCR-flagged.** Nizam al-Din Auliya is quoted that he stayed "ستر[OCR?] روز" in Shihab al-Din Umar Suhrawardi's service and received much bounty; the other murids resented it, and the shaykh answered "تم مثل لکڑی کے تر ہو اور زکریا مثل لکڑی خشک کے ہے' پس آگ خشک لکڑی کو جلد پکڑتی ہے" (p. 50, folio 46, حاشیہ 11).
  - **On the road to Multan** he met a learned qalandar, **سید عبد القدوس**, Sayyid Abd al-Quddus, of **موصل**, Mosul, who had donned the qalandars' dress at the grave of **سید جمال الدین مجرد**; "آپ نے ان کا قلندرانہ جامہ اتروایا اور عالم جذب سے ان کو عالم سلوک کو پہنچایا" — he had the qalandari garment removed and brought him from *jazb* to *suluk* (p. 51, folio 47). **شیخ جلال الدین تبریزی**, Shaikh Jalal al-Din Tabrizi, travelled with him as far as **نیشاپور**, Nishapur, where they parted (p. 51, folio 47).
  - **Arrival at Multan and the dervishes' objection.** "آپ نے ملتان پہنچ کر مستقل سکونت اختیار فرمائی" — he took **permanent residence at ملتان, Multan**; the dervishes of Multan were displeased and tried to have him leave, sending a cup filled with **milk** to say "اس شہر میں دوسرے کی گنجائش نہیں ہے", there is no room for another in this city; he placed a **flower** on the cup and sent it back, meaning his place in the city would be "جیسے کہ پھول دودھ پر ہے", as a flower is on milk; they were astonished and became his devotees (p. 51, folio 47). The pp. 1–109 worker notes that **the identical milk-and-flower idiom, and the identical claim of single-occupancy jurisdiction over a town, recur at Panipat between two other saints** (p. 94, folio 90).
  - **Death as printed, in one range only.** "آپ 7 صفر 666ھ کو بروز جمعرات جوار رحمت میں داخل ہوئے" — **7 صفر 666ھ**, 7 Safar 666 AH, on a **جمعرات**, Thursday (p. 52, folio 48, حاشیہ 15), "آپ کی عمر (100) سو سال کی تھی", his age **one hundred years** (p. 52, folio 48). The pp. 1–109 worker's own observation — **an observation of the chunk worker, not a statement of the book** — is that birth 566ھ and death 666ھ at age 100 are "internally consistent as printed" (pp. 49, 52; folios 45, 48); no interval is computed here. **No Common-Era year is printed anywhere in any range and none is computed here**; the pp. 1–109 input flags the archive row's `1267` under its Doubts D5 (p. 52, folio 48). **Silence elsewhere, not conflict**: chunk 004 records **death year: (not stated)** for pp. 74–76 (folios 70–72); pp. 110–210 records **death year: (not stated)** (pp. 113–114, 133, 135; folios 109–110, 129, 131); pp. 211–303 records **death year: (not stated)** in both mentions (p. 222, folio 218; p. 234, folio 230).
  - **Sons — the count is lost, the names are not.** "آپ کے ساتھ [OCR?] لڑکے تھے", he had [OCR?] sons — **the numeral is lost** — and seven names follow: **شیخ صدر الدین عارف، شیخ برہان الدین، شیخ ضیاء الدین، شیخ علاء الدین، شیخ شہاب الدین، شیخ قدوۃ الدین، شیخ شمس الدین** (p. 52, folio 48). **The pp. 1–109 input directs: cite the names, not a count** (p. 52, folio 48).
  - **Household habits.** He completed one Quran every night; "تحمل و بردباری' ذوق و شوق' بے ہوشی اور استغراق آپ کی نمایاں خصوصیات تھیں"; and "آپ کو سماع کا شوق تھا", **he was fond of سماع, sama** (p. 52, folio 48). He fasted much in early life but "آخری عمر میں روزانہ روزہ نہ رکھتے تھے", did not fast daily in his last years (p. 52, folio 48, حاشیہ 17).
  - **Poet.** "آپ شاعر بھی تھے", and he has a famous **منقبت, manqabat**, in honour of Ghaus al-Azam Shaikh Abd al-Qadir Jilani, "جس کے چند اشعار ذیل میں پیش کئے" — a few verses of which are presented below (p. 52, folio 48). **The chapter breaks off at that point** and the verses fall on the following page (p. 52, folio 48).
  - **The verses, on the next page** (provisionally the same chapter — the pp. 1–109 input's Doubts D4): six couplets with the refrain **"محی الدین جیلانی"** — "معلا[OCR?] جب سبحانی مقدس قطب ربانی …", "مدد یا شاہ جیلانی بریں افتادہ حیرانی …" — closing on the signature couplet **"سنگ درگاہ جیلانی بہاء الدین ملتانی / لقائے[OCR?] دین سلطانی محی الدین جیلانی"** (p. 53, folio 49).
  - **Family relations named in pp. 110–210.** He is Rukn al-Din's **grandfather (دادا)**, Bibi Rasti's **pir** and **father-in-law (خسر)** (p. 113, folio 109), and he supervised Rukn al-Din's "تعلیم و تربیت", education and upbringing (p. 114, folio 110).
  - **In pp. 211–303 he appears as the occupant of a visited mazar and as an ancestor.** "ملتان میں آپ حضرت بہاء الدین زکریا ملتانی رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کے مزار پر انوار پر حاضر ہوئے اور شیخ صدر الدین سے ملاقات کی" — in **ملتان**, Multan, Shaikh Jamali presented himself at the luminous mazar of Hazrat Baha al-Din Zakariya Multani and met **شیخ صدر الدین**, Shaikh Sadr al-Din (p. 222, folio 218); the nisba as printed there is "ملتانی" and the town as printed is "ملتان" (p. 222, folio 218). Of Shaikh Hamza Dharsawi (باب 47): "آپ حضرت شیخ بہاء الدین زکریا ملتانی رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کی اولاد ہیں" — he is of the descendants of Hazrat Shaikh Baha al-Din Zakariya Multani (p. 234, folio 230). **Caution: the inputs print "شیخ صدر الدین" at p. 222 (folio 218) with no further identification, and print "حضرت شیخ صدر الدین عارف" as son, khalifa and sajjada nashin at p. 52 (folio 48) and p. 74 (folio 70); no input joins the two, and this merge does not join them.**

- **Shrine / Darbar.** Across all three ranges the book yields two sentences that put his mazar at Multan, one that names the mazar with no town, and nothing else.
  - **His mazar is in Multan and is visited (pp. 1–109).** Maulana Hussam al-Din relates that "جب وہ ملتان میں تھے تو ایک دن وہ حضرت بہاء الدین زکریا رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کے مزار سے واپس ہو رہے تھے" — when he was in **ملتان**, Multan, he was one day **returning from the mazar** of Baha al-Din Zakariya (p. 76, folio 72). The pp. 1–109 worker records this as **the only locational statement about the shrine in that whole range, and incidental** — it occurs in another saint's chapter, as the setting of a karamat (p. 76, folio 72).
  - **His mazar is in Multan and is visited (pp. 211–303).** "ملتان میں آپ حضرت بہاء الدین زکریا ملتانی رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کے مزار پر انوار پر حاضر ہوئے" — in Multan the travelling shaykh presented himself at the **مزار پر انوار**, luminous mazar (p. 222, folio 218). **The mazar is described there only by the formula "مزار پر انوار"** (p. 222, folio 218).
  - **A mazar with no town beside it (pp. 110–210).** باب 22 records **his mazar as a site of Eid-night vigil**: "ایک سال عید کی رات کو آپ حضرت بہاء الدین زکریا رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کے مزار مبارک پر یاد الٰہی میں مشغول رہے" (p. 133, folio 129). **Placement caution: the town of the mazar is (not stated) in that sentence** (p. 133, folio 129).
  - **باب 7 states nothing whatever about his own mazar, and that silence must not be read as the book's.** The chapter breaks off at p. 52 (folio 48) inside "شعر و شاعری"; **no statement about the site, the urs, the building or the endowment of his shrine occurs in pp. 48–52** (folios 44–48), and chunk 002 warns explicitly that **the archive must not take that silence as the book's silence** — the rest of the chapter is at pp. 53–55 (folios 49–51), and that fragment likewise "states no death year, no shrine, no urs, no custodianship and no silsila" (pp. 48–55, folios 44–51).
  - **No shrine statement, no location for his own grave, no building date, no urs and no custodianship appear for him in باب 17** — "(not stated)" (pp. 113–114, folios 109–110).
  - **Nothing on buildings, urs, custodianship, endowment or a building date in pp. 211–303** (p. 222, folio 218).
  - **Urs, custodianship of his own darbar, endowment, buildings, Auqaf, visitor numbers, disputes: (not stated)** anywhere in pp. 1–109 (pp. 48–55, 74–76; folios 44–51, 70–72). The one succession statement that range carries is about the person of the successor, not about the shrine (p. 52, folio 48, حاشیہ 16) — see Practices & Events.

- **Practices & Events.**
  - **The only custodianship statement, and it is hereditary and distinguishes two kinds of succession.** "آپ کے لڑکے حضرت شیخ صدر الدین عارف آپ کے خلیفہ اور سجادہ نشین تھے" — his son **حضرت شیخ صدر الدین عارف**, Hazrat Shaikh Sadr al-Din Arif, was his **خلیفہ** and **سجادہ نشین**, khalifa and sajjada nashin — set against a separate list of five "مقتدر خلفاء", eminent khalifas, who are not sons (p. 52, folio 48, حاشیہ 16). **Succession to the seat and succession to the teaching are distinguished on one page** (p. 52, folio 48). The same relation is restated at the head of باب 11: "حضرت شیخ صدر الدین عارف اپنے والد ماجد حضرت بہاء الدین زکریا ملتانی رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کے فرزند' مرید' خلیفہ اور **جانشین** ہیں" — son, murid, khalifa and *janishin*, successor (p. 74, folio 70), and "اپنے والد … سے بیعت ہوئے اور انہیں سے خرقہ خلافت پایا" — took bai'at at his father's hand and received the **خرقہ خلافت**, khirqa of khilafat, from him (p. 74, folio 70).
  - **The five eminent khalifas who are not sons**: **حضرت سید جلال الدین سرخ بخاری**, **حضرت سید عثمان معروف بہ لعل شہباز سندی[OCR?]**, **حضرت شیخ حسن افغان**, **حضرت شیخ فخر الدین ابراہیم عراقی**, **حضرت سید صدر الدین احمد معروف بہ سید حسین** (p. 52, folio 48, حاشیہ 16). **The book gives none of them a place, a date or a shrine** (p. 52, folio 48). The pp. 1–109 input records that two of the five collide with archive rows, under its Doubts D6 and D7 (p. 52, folio 48).
  - **He conducts the succession in person (pp. 110–210).** The turban episode at p. 114 (folio 110) is his act and his words: "بابا صدر الدین! تم اس کو منع نہ کرو…" (p. 114, folio 110).
  - **A langar open to all, on the back of large-scale agriculture and trade.** "آپ کے یہاں زراعت اور تجارت بڑے پیمانے پر ہوتی تھی' ہر حال میں خدا کا شکر ادا کرتے تھے' آپ کا لنگر عام تھا' آپ بہت مخیر تھے' دنیا سے بے پروا تھے' سادگی اور قناعت کے ساتھ زندگی گزارتے تھے' بہت کم کھاتے تھے' لیکن قوی غذا کھاتے تھے' علماء کرام مشائخ عظام اور مہمانوں کی بہت عزت اور خاطر کرتے تھے" — at his place **agriculture and trade were carried on on a large scale**; he gave thanks in every condition; **his لنگر, langar, was عام, general**, open to all; he was very charitable, unconcerned with the world, lived with simplicity and contentment, ate very little but strong food, and greatly honoured the ulama, the great mashaikh and guests (p. 52, folio 48, حاشیہ 17). The pp. 1–109 worker reads this as **the clearest statement in that range of a shrine household's productive base: the langar funded by farming and commerce, not by a grant** (p. 52, folio 48).
  - **UNRECONCILED — the estate at his death, and an identical sum attached to a different event.** (a) "والد کی وفات کے بعد (70) ستر لاکھ ٹنکہ [OCR?] آپ کے حصہ میں آیا جو آپ نے فقراء کو تقسیم کر دیا" — after his father's death **ستر لاکھ ٹنکہ[OCR?]**, seventy lakh tanka[OCR?], came to Sadr al-Din Arif's share, which he distributed to the fuqara (p. 74, folio 70). (b) **An identical figure, "ستر لاکھ ٹکہ[OCR?]", seventy lakh tanka[OCR?] — note the different spelling of the unit, both OCR-flagged — is the value of the Multan distribution of the maritime offering at p. 54 (folio 50)**. **The pp. 1–109 input flags the duplication under its Doubts D8; the two statements are attached to different events and neither input reconciles them, nor does this merge** (p. 74, folio 70; p. 54, folio 50). The heir's reasoning at (a) is printed in full: "میرے والد دنیا پر غالب تھے' اس لئے مال و زر کی موجودگی ان کو کوئی نقصان نہ پہنچا سکتی تھی … اگرچہ میں بفضلہ تعالیٰ دنیا پر بیشتر غالب ہوں' مگر کبھی کبھی درجہ مساویت بھی ہو جاتا ہے' اس واسطے میں اس اندیشے سے کہ مبادا کبھی دنیا مجھ پر غالب آ جائے مطمئن نہیں ہوں' اس واسطے میں نے اسباب دنیاوی کو اپنے پاس سے دور پھینک دیا" (pp. 74–75, folios 70–71, حاشیہ 2 = سالک السالکین جلد دوم ص 526). The pp. 1–109 worker records (a) as **the largest sum in that range attached to this shrine's founding family, and the only statement about its inherited wealth** (p. 74, folio 70).
  - **A maritime offering distributed in full within thirty days.** After the ship reached **عدن**, Aden, safely, "مسافروں نے اپنا تہائی مال خواجہ کمال الدین کو **بطور نذرانہ و شکرانہ** دیا" — the passengers gave **a third of their goods** to Khwaja Kamal al-Din as **نذرانہ و شکرانہ**, nazrana and shukrana; he sent all of it, with **half his own jewels**, to Multan by his nephew; "وہ مال و جواہرات آپ نے **تیس دن کے اندر** لوگوں کو تقسیم کر دیا' اس مال کی قیمت **ستر لاکھ ٹکہ[OCR?]** تھی" — the saint distributed the whole to the people **within thirty days**, the value of the goods being **seventy lakh tanka[OCR?]** (p. 54, folio 50, note 19 → سالک السالکین جلد دوم ص 521،522). The pp. 1–109 worker reads this as **offerings arriving from Indian Ocean trade rather than from the land, with a stated disbursement timetable** (p. 54, folio 50).
  - **A grave plot asked for beside the shrine, and the successor's answer on jurisdiction.** Maulana Hussam al-Din, in Multan, returning from Baha al-Din Zakariya's mazar, conceived the wish "کہ وہ اپنی قبر کے واسطے ایک قطعہ زمین مانگیں" — to ask for a **plot of ground for his own grave**; Sadr al-Din Arif knew it by kashf and said "مجھے ایک قبر کی جگہ آپ کو دینی مشکل نہیں' اور نہ عذر ہے' لیکن آپ کے واسطے حضرت سرور عالم صلی اللہ علیہ وسلم نے ایک جگہ بدایوں میں تجویز فرمائی ہے' آپ کا مدفن اس جگہ ہوگا" — it is not difficult for me to give you a grave-place, nor do I object, but for you the Prophet has designated a place at **بدایوں**, Badaun; your burial will be there (p. 76, folio 72). The pp. 1–109 worker reads this as **the clearest statement in that range of a sajjada's disposal of burial ground inside a shrine precinct, framed as a power he holds and declines to use** (p. 76, folio 72). **Attribution of the story to a source is uncertain, flagged under that input's Doubts D19** (p. 76, folio 72).
  - **A state religious office held, as the outcome of a slander case.** "سلطان شمس الدین التمش نے نجم الدین صغریٰ[OCR?] کو شیخ الاسلام کے عہدے سے علیحدہ کر دیا … اس جھوٹے بہتان کی سزا میں نجم الدین صغریٰ کو اپنے عہدے سے دست بردار ہونا پڑا۔ نجم الدین صغریٰ کے بجائے آپ شیخ الاسلام مقرر ہوئے" — Sultan Shams al-Din Altamsh removed **نجم الدین صغریٰ[OCR?]** from the office of **شیخ الاسلام**, Shaikh al-Islam; as punishment for the false slander he had to relinquish it, and **in his place Bahauddin Zakariya was appointed Shaikh al-Islam** (pp. 51–52, folios 47–48, حاشیہ 14). The pp. 1–109 worker records this as **the most direct statement of state office held by a figure the archive carries**; the entry gives it its own headed section, "عہدہ" (pp. 51–52, folios 47–48).
  - **He acts twice as an authority in باب 2, not as a subject.** He rebukes Jalal al-Din Tabrizi for not naming Shihab al-Din Suhrawardi before Farid al-Din Attar, is displeased and separates from him at Nishapur (p. 35, folio 31, حاشیہ 6); and at **دہلی**, Delhi, chosen as **ثالث**, arbitrator, of a shariah tribunal, he lifts the accused saint's shoes in his own hands and declares "میرے اوپر واجب ہے کہ شیخ جلال کی خاک پا کو اپنی آنکھوں کا سرمہ بناؤں' کیوں کہ وہ سات برس تک سفر و خضر[OCR?] میں میرے پیر و مرشد کی خدمت میں مقیم رہے ہیں" — it is obligatory on me to make the dust of Shaikh Jalal's feet the kohl of my eyes, because for **seven years** in travel and residence[OCR?] he stayed in the service of my pir and murshid — and then examines the woman, who confesses the whole plot (pp. 36–37, folios 32–33, حاشیہ 9). **The accuser had counted on the earlier estrangement**: "ان کو خیال تھا کہ حضرت بہاء الدین زکریا رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کی آپ سے نیشاپور میں کشیدگی ہو گئی تھی" (p. 36, folio 32).
  - **The tribunal itself, in which he is the pivot**: "جب سلطان التمش کے پاس شکایت پہنچی تو اس نے تمام مشاہیر اور مشائخ کو دہلی بلایا۔ دو سو سے زیادہ اولیائے کرام دہلی آئے۔ جمعہ کی نماز کے بعد سب مسجد منار میں جمع ہوئے" — **more than two hundred** auliya came to Delhi; after the Friday prayer all assembled in the **مسجد منار**, Masjid Minar; the Sultan let the accuser name the arbitrator; when the accused entered "سب آپ کی تعظیم کے لئے کھڑے ہو گئے", all stood in respect, and the Sultan said "جب ثالث نے اتنی عزت کی تو پھر معاملہ طے ہو گیا", when the arbitrator has shown such honour the matter is decided (p. 36, folio 32, حاشیہ 9).
  - **He is quoted assessing another saint**: "اگر احمد کی مشغولی وزن کی جائے تو دس صوفیوں کی مشغولی کے برابر ہو گی", if Ahmad's absorption were weighed it would equal that of ten Sufis (p. 46, folio 42).
  - **He corresponds with Baba Farid.** He writes "میان ما و شما عشق بازی است", between us and you is love-play, to which Farid replies "میان ما و شما عشق ہست و بازی نیست", between us and you is love, and no play (pp. 63–64, folios 59–60, note 20). He is also among the mashaikh whose company Farid benefited from (p. 59, folio 55, note 13).
  - **He confers khilafat on Jalal al-Din Surkh Bukhari** the same day as the hail karamat: "آپ نے ان کو اسی روز خرقہ خلافت سے مشرف فرمایا" (p. 54, folio 50).
  - **He founds the Uch line from Multan by an order of residence (pp. 110–210).** Jalal al-Din Surkh Bukhari became his murid at Multan, "ان ہی سے خرقہ خلافت پایا' اپنے پیرو مرشد کے حکم سے اوچہ میں سکونت اختیار کی" — received the **خرقہ خلافت**, khirqa of khilafat, from him and **took up residence at اوچہ, Uch, by his pir's order** (p. 133, folio 129). The pp. 110–210 worker reads the settlement at Uch as **presented as an instruction issued from Multan, not a choice** (p. 133, folio 129).
  - **His khirqa is a heritable object crossing two generations of another family at another shrine.** Makhdum Jahaniyan "حضرت شیخ بہاء الدین زکریا رحمۃ اللہ علیہ کا خرقہ اپنے والد سید احمد کبیر سے پہنا" — wore Baha al-Din Zakariya's khirqa, received from his own father **سید احمد کبیر**, Sayyid Ahmad Kabir (p. 135, folio 131).
  - **He names the child and predicts him before birth**, rising for his pregnant daughter-in-law: "یہ تعظیم اس بچے کی ہے۔ جو تیرے پیٹ میں ہے۔ وہ چراغ خاندان و شمع دو دمان کا ہو گا" (p. 113, folio 109, footnote 1 → "سالک السالکین (جلد دوم) ص 527").
  - **Two teachings on wealth**, from the fragment at p. 53 (note 18 → سالک السالکین جلد دوم ص 515): "مال دنیا کتنا ہی کیوں نہ ہو' تاہم قلیل ہے اور سانپ کی صحبت اس شخص کو نقصان پہنچاتی ہے' جو افسوں اس کا نہ جانتا ہو' میرے نزدیک مال دنیا کی کوئی حقیقت نہیں ہے" — however much worldly wealth there is, it is still little, and the company of a snake harms one who does not know its charm; in my view worldly wealth has no reality; and "فقیروں کے نزدیک عدم اور وجود مال دنیا کا یکساں ہے' نہ جانے کا غم' نہ آنے کی خوشی" — for faqirs the absence and presence of worldly wealth are the same, no grief at its going, no joy at its coming (p. 53, folio 49).
  - **Aqwal zarin** (same fragment): "زہد تین چیزیں ہیں' جس میں یہ نہیں وہ زاہد نہیں" — zuhd is three things and whoever lacks them is no ascetic: knowing the truth of the world and letting go of it; serving the Lord and keeping to his adab; longing for and seeking the hereafter. And: safety lies in little food, the soul's safety in leaving people, and the faith's safety in the prayer of the Best of Creation (p. 53, folio 49).
  - **A mosque with a khadim, prayer rows and a courtyard** used as the stage of the hail karamat: he "نے اپنے ایک خادم کو مسجد میں بھیجا اور یہ تاکید فرمائی صفیں لپیٹ کر مسجد کے صحن میں جھاڑو دیں" — sent a **خادم**, khadim, with orders to roll up the prayer rows and sweep the courtyard, and himself came at **ظہر**, Zuhr (pp. 53–54, folios 49–50).
  - **Multan stands as one stop on an itinerary of shrine visitation (pp. 211–303)**, between the Haramain and مصر (Egypt), بغداد (Baghdad), بیت المقدس, روم, شام, عراق, عرب و عجم, آزربائجان, گیلان, مازندران and خراسان (p. 222, folio 218, حاشیہ 3 = المشاہیر صفحہ 209). At the same stop a **living successor** is met, شیخ صدر الدین (p. 222, folio 218).

- **Legends & Miracles.**
  - **The khirqa dream and its waking confirmation.** He saw in a dream a house of light with the Prophet present, his pir standing respectfully, and many khirqas hanging on a rope; he was summoned, his hand taken, and the Prophet said "اے عمر! اس خرقہ کو شیخ بہاء الدین غوث العالم کو پہنا دو", O Umar, clothe Shaikh Bahauddin Ghaus al-Alam in this khirqa; in the morning the pir called him, "آپ نے مکان اسی طرح کا پایا' جیسا کہ خواب میں دیکھا تھا", he found the house exactly as in the dream, and the same khirqa was taken down "طنطاب [OCR?] سے اتار کر" and put on him by hand, the pir disclaiming any authority of his own: "یہ سب خرقے سرور عالم ﷺ کے ہیں' میں تو درمیان میں صرف ایک واسطہ ہوں' بے اجازت سرور عالم ﷺ کے کسی کو نہیں پہنا سکتا" (pp. 50–51, folios 46–47, حواشی 12–13). After conferring it the pir "آپ کو ملتان جانے کی تاکید فرمائی", enjoined him to go to Multan (p. 51, folio 47) — **the book's stated reason for his presence at Multan** (p. 51, folio 47).
  - **Memory of the primordial covenant**, claimed as a schoolboy: he remembered events from the moment God said "الست بربکم" (p. 49, folio 45, حاشیہ 9).
  - **The flower on the milk** (p. 51, folio 47), told as a wordless exchange rather than a marvel, ending with the dervishes of Multan becoming his devotees (p. 51, folio 47).
  - **Hail over a mosque in Multan and nowhere outside it.** Under کشف و کرامات: when Jalal al-Din Surkh Bukhari came to Multan and pined for Bukhara's weather, "آپ کو یہ بات کشف سے معلوم ہو گئی" — the saint knew it by **کشف, kashf**; he had the mosque swept, a cloud appeared, heavy rain fell and hailstones, "مسجد سے باہر نہ بارش ہوئی اور نہ اولے", and outside the mosque there was neither rain nor hail; at Zuhr he came smiling and asked "کہئے سید! اولے ملتان کے بہتر ہیں یا برف بخارا کا", to which the Sayyid answered "اس صورت میں تو اولے ملتان کے سو درجہ بہتر ہیں" (pp. 53–54, folios 49–50).
  - **A pir appearing on a storm-bound ship.** Khwaja Kamal al-Din Masud Shirazi called on him in the storm; "آپ جہاز پر نمودار ہوئے' سب مسافروں نے آپ کو بخوبی دیکھا", the saint appeared on the ship and all the passengers saw him plainly, gave tidings of safety, and the ship reached **عدن**, Aden, well; the nephew who carried the goods to Multan recognised him at once "اور اسی لباس میں پایا' جس میں انہوں نے آپ کو جہاز پر دیکھا تھا", in the very dress in which he had seen him on the ship (p. 54, folio 50, note 19).

- **Significance.**
  - **The book's interest in him is institutional as much as mystical**: باب 7 devotes a whole headed section, **"عہدہ"**, to the office of Shaikh al-Islam (pp. 51–52, folios 47–48) and another, **"خلفاء"**, to succession (p. 52, folio 48).
  - **Descent is the one thing the author treats as settled**: after printing four accounts of the lineage he writes "آپ ہاشمی ہیں اور آپ کے ہاشمی ہونے میں کوئی شک و شبہ نہیں" (p. 48, folio 44, حاشیہ 5). Nowhere else in pp. 1–109 does he adjudicate (pp. 48–55, 74–76; folios 44–51, 70–72).
  - **Agreement with the archive row, stated as agreement and not as new evidence**: the row's location (Multan) agrees with "ملتان پہنچ کر مستقل سکونت اختیار فرمائی" (p. 51, folio 47) and with the mazar statement (p. 76, folio 72); the row's silsila (Suhrawardi) agrees with "مرید اور خلیفہ" of Shihab al-Din Umar Suhrawardi (p. 48, folio 44). **No conflict on either field is recorded anywhere in pp. 1–109** (pp. 48–55, 74–76; folios 44–51, 70–72). The row's `figure_died` **1267** is neither confirmed nor contradicted (p. 52, folio 48, the pp. 1–109 input's Doubts D5).
  - **Within pp. 110–210 he functions as the origin-point of two archive rows at once**: the Multan sajjada transmitted to his grandson (p. 114, folio 110) and the Uch settlement ordered for his khalifa (p. 133, folio 129).
  - **Within pp. 211–303 the mazar's function in the book is to be a station on a journey, not a subject** — that range's worker records it as "a tazkirah-level record of shrine tourism as a stage in a shaykh's formation" (p. 222, folio 218).
  - **A second, distinct "بہاء الدین" occupies باب 41 of the pp. 211–303 chunk (p. 214, folio 210) and must not be merged with him** (p. 214, folio 210).

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **D4. The attribution of pp. 53–55 (folios 49–51) to باب 7 is provisional, though two chunks now
  corroborate it.** Chunk 003 mapped the fragment to **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya** on the strength of
  the takhallus in the closing couplet, **"بہاء الدین ملتانی"** (p. 53, folio 49), plus the Multan setting
  of the karamat (pp. 53–54, folios 49–50), and asked a human to confirm from chunk_002 which chapter runs
  into p. 53. **Consolidating the two chunks: chunk 002 records باب 7 breaking off at p. 52 (folio 48)
  inside "شعر و شاعری", immediately after announcing a manqabat in honour of Ghaus al-Azam Shaikh Abd
  al-Qadir Jilani "جس کے چند اشعار ذیل میں پیش کئے"; chunk 003 records p. 53 (folio 49) opening with a
  qasida in praise of Abd al-Qadir Jilani, refrain "محی الدین جیلانی", signed "بہاء الدین ملتانی". The two
  records are consistent and the contents list places باب 8 at printed ۵۲ = p. 56 (p. 15), leaving pp. 53–55
  inside باب 7.** **This is a cross-chunk inference, not a statement the book makes on any single page —
  a human should still read the running head or the chapter opening on the PDF before the fragment's
  material is fixed to the row.** The fragment itself states **no death year, no shrine, no urs, no
  custodianship and no silsila**.

- **D5. shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya: a field to reconcile, not a conflict.** The book gives birth
  "27 رمضان 566ھ" and death "7 صفر 666ھ" with age **one hundred** (pp. 49, 52; folios 45, 48) —
  **internally consistent** — while the archive row's `figure_died` reads **1267**. **No Common-Era year
  is stated anywhere in the range**, so a human should confirm the era conversion rather than let either
  field be rewritten. Chunk 004 separately records **death year: (not stated)** and **silsila: (not
  stated)** for its pages (pp. 74–76, folios 70–72): **silence, not corroboration.** The row's location
  and silsila agree with the book (pp. 48, 51, 76; folios 44, 47, 72); **no conflict is recorded on any
  field.**

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "پنجاب"; refers to: the reign of "شہنشاہ اکبر", the date printed "بروز شنبہ بتاریخ 2 محرم 980ھ"; quoted source — the sentence carries حاشیہ 5 of باب 49, "انوار العارفین (فارسی) صفحہ 367", p. 245, folio 241): in باب 49, on حضرت شیخ جلال الدین تھانیسری, under the heading "اکبر کی باریابی": "شہنشاہ اکبر بروز شنبہ بتاریخ 2 محرم 980ھ کو مزار حکیم کی بغاوت کو فرد کرنے کی غرض سے پنجاب جاتے ہوئے تھانیسر میں رکا" — Punjab is the destination of an imperial campaign against a revolt; the stop, and the shaikh's خانقاہ, are at تھانیسر, which is not said to be in پنجاب. "مزار حکیم" and "فرد" are printed so and carry no flag; the sense calls for a person's name and a verb of suppressing, so check the page image before quoting (p. 243, folio 239).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "between 1748 and l75I" [text-layer?] [1751]; quoted source: Sir Alfred Lyall, *History of India*): Ahmad Shah of the Abdali Afghans, after Nadir Shah's assassination, rode east "to capture Afghanistan and from that base he seized the whole of the Punjab between 1748 and l75I" [text-layer?] [1751] (p. 17, folio 8).
- (book's word: "the Punjab rivers"; refers to: the centuries before "the mid­eighteenth century", when the north-west barriers were "for ever closed by the growth of the kingdom of Afghanistan ... and the rise of the S ikhs" [text-layer?] [Sikhs]; book's historical prose): invaders by-passed Sind: "the wandering hordes which poured over the frontier barriers of the nor th-west [text-layer?] [north-west] ... passed rapidly to the more prom ising [text-layer?] [promising] fields of the Punjab rive rs [text-layer?] [rivers] and the Ganget ic [text-layer?] [Gangetic] plain in their assaults on t he pen insular land of Hindustan" (p. 22, folio 13).
- (book's word: "the plains of the Punj ab" [text-layer?] [Punjab], "the Punjab"; refers to: 1758 and 1760; book's historical prose): when the Company re-entered Sind in 1758 "Afghans, Sikhs and Marathas were at each other's throats in the plains of the Punj ab" [text-layer?] [Punjab] (p. 46, folio 37); in 1760 "The Afghan and Maratha armies were threatening each other in the Punjab" (p. 70, folio 61).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, the period of the chapter; book's historical prose reporting Sarkar, *Moghul Administration* (Second Series)): "Sarkar has also remarked that, with the Punjab, Sind was the home of the leather industry" (p. 107, folio 98).
- (book's word: "the Punj ab" [text-layer?] [Punjab]; refers to: "the seventeenth century"; book's historical prose): against the rule that Moghul public money went to tombs and palaces rather than public works, "J:tere [text-layer?] [There] is, however, an exception to this generalization in the seventeenth century canal enterprise in the Punj ab [text-layer?] [Punjab] and improvements carried out on the old · Delhi Jumna canal"; the book does not say where in the Punjab (p. 122, folio 113).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "Punjab"; refers to: "1 756-8" and "1 758" as printed (years split by a space [text-layer?]), in the column "Events in Indian History"; chronological appendix): "Sikhs rise in the Punjab" (p. 201, folio 192); "Marathas occupy Punjab" (p. 202, folio 193). The book does not bound "the Punjab" here.
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "a Punjab tale"; refers to: period not stated, before the Risalo's composition; author's argument): "The story of Suhini and Mehar is a Punjab tale which has found a home in Sind and it must have come into Sind through the immigration from the Punjab of those Hindu races which form an important element in the population, as has been made clear in the Census of India volume for 1931 relating to the Bombay Presidency" (p. 270, folio 261); of the Risalo's stories, "one of therii frorn t4e Punj a.9" [text-layer?] [one of them from the Punjab] (p. 271, folio 262). Punjab figures only as the source of a migration and a tale; the statement is chiefly about Sind's population.

## From Annemarie Schimmel, Pain and Grace

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/schimmel_pain_and_grace.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book prints him `"Baha?uddin Zakariya"[OCR?]` [Baha'uddin Zakariya] and "Baha 'uddin Zakariya";
the index has "Baha ?'uddin[OCR?] Zakariya Multani 4, 21" (p. 301, folio 301), **identified by the
nisba "Multani"**. **No tomb, shrine, ʿurs, custodian or building date at Multan** — all **(not
stated)**.

- **Saint** — **Silsila, seat and a dated floruit of association.** "the Suhrawardi master, set-tled in
  Multan, at the border of Sind and the Panjab: one of the leading Persian mystical poets, Fakhruddin
  Iraqi, stayed with him for 25 years" (p. 8, folio 4). **Death year as printed: "(d. c. 1265)" —
  note the explicit "c."** The archive row gives 1267 — see Doubts. **The archive row's `Suhrawardi`
  is confirmed twice**: "the Suhrawardi master" (p. 8, folio 4) and he "established the Suhrawardi
  center at the northernmost border of the country, in Multan" (p. 25, folio 21).
- **Significance** — **The book places Multan inside Sind**, calling it "the northernmost border of
  the country" (p. 25, folio 21), not in the Punjab. **This bears directly on how the row's region is
  described.**
- **Significance** — **His order's reach and its comparative discipline.** "The Suhrawardiyya, not as
  austere in their practices as the early Chishtiyya, sent their members throughout the coun-try and
  gained disciples even in Eastern Bengal" (p. 8, folio 4). **The only characterisation of the
  Suhrawardiyya in the book, and it is attached to him.** The index's order-run is "Suhrawardiyya 4,
  21, 204" (p. 308, folio 308) — **three figures, the third being Makhdum Nuh of Hala.**
- **Apparatus** — "Multan 4, 13, 21, 24" (p. 306, folio 306) shares both of his index page figures.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Sind and the Panjab", "the Panjab"; refers to: the conquests "first by Nadir Shah and then by Ahmad Shah Durrani", and "the days of Fariduddin Shakarganj, the Chishti saint of Pakpattan (d. 1265)" — as printed; book's historical prose): "The countries which were conquered first by Nadir Shah and then by Ahmad Shah Durrani, Sind and the Panjab, could likewise boast of a rich literary heritage. In the Panjab, popular mystical poetry may have emerged as early as in the days of Fariduddin Shakarganj"; "Numerous folk songs and ballads existed but were never committed to writing" (p. 16, folio 12).
- (book's word: "the province"; refers to: "the late 17th and the 18th century" — as printed; book's historical prose): "The golden age of Muslim Panjabi poetry is the late 17th and the 18th century, e.g., the time when the province was invaded one time after the other by Mahrattas, Sikhs, and, in between, by the liberating Muslim armies" (p. 17, folio 13).
- (book's word: "a Panjabi"; refers to: "even today", present day of writing; author's argument): of Warith Shah's *Hir Ranjha* (composed "around 1794"): "even today a Panjabi will be deeply touched when this moving story is sung" (p. 17, folio 13).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Nadir Shah's invasion, "before he reached Delhi in 1739" — as printed; book's historical prose): Nadir Shah "first conquered Sind and the Panjab before he reached Delhi in 1739"; Ahmad Shah Abdali's influence "on the scene in Sind is not as important as it was in the northern areas of present day Pakistan" (p. 30, folio not stated).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "1750" and after, the reign of Ahmad Shah of Delhi, to "1754" — as printed; book's historical prose): as the Mughal empire "had broken into pieces", "Sikh threatened the Panjab"; "Nadir Shah's successor, Ahmad Shah Durrani, invaded the Panjab several times, coming from his native Afghanistan" (p. 50, folio 48).
- (book's word: "Panjabi", "rural provinces"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's lifetime and "later" poets, period otherwise not stated; author's argument): similarities with "Panjabi mystical folk poetry, which reached its apex with Bullhe Shah during exactly the same period" are "conspicuous"; Siraiki "forms the transition to Panjabi; thus, the imagery of both, or rather all three, linguistic areas is very closely related, as is to be expected from the inhabitants of predominatly [OCR?] [predominantly] rural provinces" (p. 154, folio 154).
- (book's word: "both Sind and the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "Sohni is one of the favorite folk tales in both Sind and the Panjab" (p. 156, folio 156).
- (book's word: "the poetry of Sind and the Panjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument): "A topic which is not rarely found in the poetry of Sind and the Panjab is the spinning of cotton, one of the most important occupations of women"; the Kapa'iti [printed "Kdpd ?it?" [OCR?]] melodies are "traditional folk melodies sung by the womenfolk while spinning" (p. 160, folio 160).
- (book's word: "the Panjab", "Panjabi contemporaries"; refers to: Shah Abdul Latif's lifetime (the book dates him "1689-1752"); author's argument): Latif "stands before us as the representative of that type of the rural mystical tradition in which his contemporaries in the Panjab and, to a certain extent, in the Pashto speaking areas were rooted"; "Shah Abdul Latif, as did his Panjabi contemporaries and his successors in Sind, accepted the Hindus who were striving on the way to perfection as true pilgrims in the eternal Light" (p. 267, folio 267).

## From Christopher Shackle, Bulleh Shah: Sufi Lyrics

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/shackle_bulleh_shah_sufi_lyrics.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's forms: "the great saint Bahaval Haq", "Baha ud Din Zakariya of Multan", "Bahāval Haq".

- **Saint** — "bahaval (Bahāval Haq) title given to the Sufi saint Baha ud Din Zakariya of Multan."
  (p. 198, folio 173). Death year, order and lineage are **(not stated)** anywhere in the volume.
- **Shrine / Darbar** — **the only tomb in the book described as a place people visit.** "The
  popular title of Baha ud Din Zakariya of Multan, one of the Five Pirs. This invitation to visit
  his tomb in Multan, which is a major center of pilgrimage, strikes a strange note, and the verse
  may be a later interpolation." (p. 186, folio 161, n. 5 on 122). No building, date, custodian or
  endowment is given — (not stated).
- **Practices & Events** — the ziyarat is put in Hir's mouth: "Come, Ranjha, let us go to Multan,
  to pay homage to the great saint Bahaval Haq.5 Let us gain our desire immediately. My heart longs
  for the lord" (p. 123, folio 98). **This is the only pilgrimage to a named saint's place anywhere
  in the 157 lyrics.**
- **Significance** — he is one of the Five Pirs: "Five great saints who are believed collectively
  to possess special powers… the Five Pirs (panj pīr) are named as the legendary Khwaja Khizr,
  Farid Shakarganj of Pakpattan, Baha ud Din Zakariya of Multan, Jalal ud Din Makhdum Jahaniyan of
  Uch, and Lal Shahbaz Qalandar of Sehwan." (p. 186, folio 161, n. 2 on 122). The Five Pirs are
  invoked in the verse as protectors and as recipients of propitiation: "The Five Pirs2 protect my
  beloved, they search for him among people" (p. 122, folio 97); "I belong to the yogi, after
  propitiating the Five Pirs.13" (p. 136, folio 111).
- **A textual caution the book supplies itself.** The verse naming his tomb is judged possibly
  spurious (p. 186, folio 161, n. 5 on 122) — the archive should not cite the ziyarat without that
  qualifier.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: Bullhe Shah's lifetime — the book gives "Bullhe Shah (d. 1758)"; book's historical prose): "He lived at a time of great social upheaval in Panjab,1 as the central authority of the declining Mughal empire came under increasing challenge from armed rebellion by the Sikhs and from local notables establishing their auton-omy" [text-layer?] [autonomy]; the "1" fused to "Panjab," is an endnote marker (note 1: "See further Nijjar 1972, Alam 1986."), not a figure; "these unsettled conditions are occasionally re­flected [text-layer?] [reflected] in the poetry" (p. 8, folio vii).
- (book's word: "eigh­teenth-century Panjab" [text-layer?] [eighteenth-century Panjab]; refers to: "early eigh­teenth-century" [text-layer?] [eighteenth-century], as the book states; author's argument): "Bullhe Shah’s critique of the social upheavals in early eigh­teenth-century [text-layer?] [eighteenth-century] Panjab, which is so interesting to many modern readers, represents only a very small, if vividly expressed, part of his poetry" (p. 15, folio xiv).
- (book's word: "Panjab", "Panjabi society"; refers to: the time of kāfī 34, "The twelfth century of the Hijri era", which the note says "corresponds to the eighteenth century C.E. (1100 A.H. = 1688 C.E.)" — digits as printed; editor's note): the kāfī's allusive verses "appear to refer to a separation of Bullhe Shah from Shah Inayat due to the troubled conditions then prevailing in Panjab"; the note compares kāfī 4 "for the allusions to the disturbed state of contemporary Panjabi society" (pp. 165–166, folios 140–141).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: the poet's own time, "The twelfth century" as the verse puts it — see the previous bullet for the note's reading; translation of the poet's verse, kāfī 34 — FLAG: verse register, kept because it characterises the region): "The twelfth century4 has come with gaping mouth. The door of doom and torment has opened, and Panjab is in a bad state. It has been struck by fear of the pit of hell." (the "4" fused to "century" is an endnote marker) (p. 52, folio 27).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: period not stated in the note; editor's note on kāfī 143, n. 35): a verse is "Taken to be an allusion to the Sikhs, whose guerilla horsemen ultimately destroyed the Mughal authority in Panjab" (p. 193, folio 168).
- (book's word: "eighteenth-century Panjab"; refers to: the eighteenth century, as the book states; editor's note on kāfī 86, n. 3): "The Ranghars were a tribe of Muslim Rajputs who achieved prominence in eighteenth-century Panjab, and who were proverbial for their arrogance" (p. 179, folio 154).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: "the Mughal period and beyond", following "The Muslim conquest of northern India" — no date given; book's historical prose): "The Muslim conquest of northern India extended this Persianate cultural world to Panjab, where Persian remained the dominant literary language of the ruling elite through the Mughal period and beyond. A strong Sufi presence was rapidly established with the arrival of charismatic fig­ures [text-layer?] [figures] associated with the main Sufi orders" (p. 11, folio x).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: the Sufi poets "who lived at different times", and their collection "only in the late nineteenth century"; book's historical prose): "the lyrics of Bullhe Shah and the other Sufi poets, who lived at different times and in different areas of Panjab, were never systematically preserved by an or­ga­nized [text-layer?] [organized] faith community, and they were for the most part rec­orded [text-layer?] [recorded] and assembled in printed collections only in the late nineteenth century" (pp. 9–10, folios viii–ix).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: "the British conquest of Panjab in the 1840s" and after, as the book states; book's historical prose): "Only with the British conquest of Panjab in the 1840s did the literary culture of the Sufi tradition come to be overlaid by new patterns of modernity"; Persian "was quite rapidly replaced as the language of education, administra-tion [text-layer?] [administration], and elite literature by Urdu and Eng­lish [text-layer?] [English]"; "Publishers in Lahore" brought the older Sufi poetry "into the new print culture" (pp. 11–12, folios x–xi).
- (book's word: "Panjab"; refers to: "twentieth-century" understandings and "the new middle class of the colonial period", as the book states; book's historical prose): "In Panjab the formulation of nationalist un­der­stand­ings [text-layer?] [understandings] was no simple pro­cess [text-layer?] [process], given the complexities created by the simultaneous currency of three closely related languages, Urdu, Hindi, and Panjabi, whose differences were exploited to sharpen the rival cultural identities of Muslims, Hindus, and Sikhs, respectively" (p. 12, folio xi).
- (book's word: "western Panjab"; refers to: the setting of the Hir–Ranjha legend, period not stated; book's prose on the poet's legendary material — the book itself marks the unit as "western"): "The greatest of these local legends is the story of Hir and Ranjha, which is set in the world of the Jat pastoral tribes of western Panjab" (p. 20, folio xix).

## From Taufiq Rafat, Bulleh Shah: A Selection

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rafat_bulleh_shah_selection.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- **Saint**
  - **Named once, as founder of the Suhrawardiya, with Multan attached in the same sentence.** "His
    reverential reference to Multan as a holy city and to Bahauddin Zakria, the founder of the
    Suhrawardiya school is frequent." (p. 39, folio 19). **The book spells him "Bahauddin Zakria"
    against the archive row's "Bahauddin Zakariya"** — see *Doubts*. **No death year, no tomb, no
    shrine** — (not stated).
- **Practices & Events**
  - **His order given an economic character, set against the Qadiriya's — the most archive-usable
    sentence about institutions in the whole book.** "The Suhrawardiya tradition of patronage is
    diametrically opposed to that of the Qadiriya who practise asceticism and are given to
    assimilation of local cults." (p. 39, folio 19).
- **Significance**
  - **His city as an institutional seat.** "Takht Hazara, the birth place of Ranjha, is a mystical
    idyll which attains the same status in the eyes of Bulleh Shah as the Prem Nagar of bbakti
    [OCR?] [bhakti] tradition and Multan as the seat of the Suhrawardiya school of mysticism."
    (pp. 38–39, folios 18–19).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: Bulleh Shah's lifetime, "a time of great upheaval. The Mughal Empire was breaking up" — as the Translator's Note states it; Translator's Note, Taufiq Rafat): "the Punjab was being persistently rent by petty chiefs and marauders"; "The full bitterness of Bulleh Shah’s ‘Punjab has gone to the dogs’ will only come through against this backdrop" (p. 16, folio xvi).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: Bulleh Shah's adult life, from Aurangzeb's death "in 1707" (margin heading "End of Mughal rule 1707.") — as the book states; book's historical prose, Introduction): Bulleh Shah's "depressive outlook was in no small measure produced by the political situation in the Punjab"; he was "deeply bruised" by the upheavals "taking place in the Punjab and was depressed by the sufferings of the common man"; "After Aurangzeb, the halcyon days that the Punjab had enjoyed quickly came to an end. Already badly defended, the North-Western region of the empire became increasingly subject to foreign" invasions "and inner centrifugal quarrels" (p. 26, folio 6).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the region", "the Pun-jabi of today"; refers to: from "The period of Muhammad Shah" (accession "in 1719" as printed) "into the 19th century", with a claim about the present day; author's argument, Introduction): that period "coincided with the beginning of the misfortunes of the Punjab. The decay of Mughal administration gave rise to small local tyrannies which lasted into the 19th century and formed the character of the" inhabitants "of the region"; the crisis of character in the Punjabi of today "is traceable to this period of duress in the history of his land" (pp. 26–27, folios 6–7; the sentence carries note 4 — see the Janus bullet below).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: after "The invasion of Nadir Shah in 1738" and Abdali's invasions "in 1748 and then a third in 1752," — as printed; book's historical prose, Introduction): "Marauding Sikh bands formed themselves into robber baronies and imposed their own system of extortion on the Punjab farmer"; Abdali's "treatment of the Punjab weakened the government in Lahore and delivered the various doabs into the hands of the Sikh" confederacy "called the misis" [text-layer?] [misls]; Bulleh Shah "was fated to see a second invasion of the Punjab by Ahmad Shah Abdali in 1748 and then a third in 1752," (p. 27, folio 7).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "1758", the year of Bulleh Shah's death, as printed; book's historical prose, Introduction): "As Bulleh Shah lay dying in 1758 at the age of 78, the rape of the Punjab was proceeding apace" (p. 28, folio 8).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the province"; refers to: "In the years to come" after Aurangzeb's period, with Banda Bairagi "(1670-1716)", Abdul Samad Khan, Zakriya Khan, Nadir Shah and "Shahidganj in 1746" — as printed; book's historical prose, Introduction): "In the years to come, when plunder became routine in the Punjab, Bulleh Shah had no choice of factions. The Sikh marauders under Banda Bairagi (1670-1716) were to lay waste the whole of the province excluding Lahore and Qasur, imposing bone-crushing taxation .on [text-layer?] [on] the common peasant for the raising of armies on both sides" (pp. 42–43, folios 22–23).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated — the passage runs from "The ancient cities of the Indus" to the present; author's argument, Introduction): "In terms of written sources, the Punjab and indeed the whole of the Indus basin, has always" seemed "a wasteland to scholars" (pp. 46–47, folios 26–27).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated — said of the survival of Baba Farid's and Shah Husain's poetry through Sikh collectors, the latter "in the 1940’s"; author's argument, Introduction): "A lack of intellectual tradition among the Muslims of the Punjab accounts for this phillistine [text-layer?] [philistine] indifference to the classics. Bulleh Shah was treated no differently" (p. 47, folio 27).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: after Dr. Nazeer Ahmad's selection "In 1976", i.e. the present of the Introduction; author's argument, Introduction): "After this the Punjab awaits a proper" scholarly "assessment of its greatest poet, a tryst that may never take place given the regional antecedents in the realm of letters" (p. 49, folio 29).
- (book's word: "Punjabi literature in Pakistan", "the Punjabis"; refers to: the present of the Introduction, date not stated in the passage; author's argument, Introduction — language-centred, kept because it states a condition of the Punjabis in Pakistan in general): "Punjabi literature in Pakistan is in a state of neglect. The language has become a purely spoken medium and the Punjabis are no longer able to" decipher "it in the written form" (p. 50, folio 30).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "Punjabi"; refers to: "Viewpoint weekly (July 23, 1981)", present day; quoted source — the columnist "Janus", "‘Are you a Punjabi?’", a satirical questionnaire "in which ‘c’ stands for the Punjabi response", which Notes on the Introduction note 4 says "has accurately enumerated the ingredients of this lapse from character"): item (5), "You have lived in the Punjab all your life, but suddenly you come to know that your ancestors had come here from Central Asia", with the "Punjabi response" "(c) What the hell! I am a Bokhari and superior to all the low stock inhabiting the Punjab"; item (10), "All regional ‘languages [text-layer?] [languages] in Pakistan are taught in schools except Punjabi. All regional languages in Pakistan possess dictionaries except Punjabi"; item (11), "All regional personalities are moulded by a deep love of their idiom. A Sindhi will read Sindhi poetry and can tell you who is the greatest living Sindhi poet; so will the Pathan and the Baluch, Except [text-layer?] [Baluch. Except], of course, the Punjabi" (p. 236, folio 216; pp. 237–238, folios 217–218).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period as the verse gives it, "This century"; translation of the poet's verse — the poem "COME SEE ME ONCE IN A WHILE"; flagged: a verse line, kept only because it says something about the region and the Translator's Note (p. 16) reads it as political): "Now hell has opened its bogs. / Punjab has gone to the dogs. / This hell is the nethermost hell." … "This century cramps our style." (p. 149, folio 129).

## From Waris Shah, Hir and Ranjha (Usborne tr.)

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/waris_shah_hir_ranjha.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book spells him **"Zakana" [text-layer?] [Zakaria?]** (p. 10, folio 9), **"Zalkaria"
[text-layer?] [Zakaria]** in the verse (p. 11, folio 10), **"Baha-ud-Din Zakauia" [text-layer?]
[Zakaria]** and **"Zakaria"** in note 2 (p. 11, folio 10), **"Baha-ud-Din Zakaria"** in note 10
(p. 16, folio 15), and the bare **"Pit [text-layer?] [Pir] Bahauddin"** (p. 50, folio 49).
**"Zakariya" in the archive's spelling never appears; neither does a death date, an order, a shrine
fabric or a custodian** — the row's d. 1267 and Suhrawardi are (not stated).

- **Saint**
  - *Multan given as the place of burial, by the annotator.* Note 2 to Ch. 7: "'Zakaria' is a
    reference to **Baha-ud-Din `Zakauia` [text-layer?] [Zakaria] of Multan, a famous `mint`
    [text-layer?] [saint] who is `busied` [text-layer?] [buried] in that city**." (p. 11, folio 10).
    Note 10 repeats "Baha-ud-Din Zakaria … of Multan" (p. 16, folio 15).
  - *His epithet in the verse straddles a chunk boundary and completes as Multan.* "Then Shahbaz
    Qalandar, the holy saint of Uch,2 and `Zakana` [text-layer?] [Zakaria], saint of" (p. 10, folio 9)
    → "**Multan**, and Sayyid Jalal of Bukhara, whom men also call Makhdum Jahanian." (p. 11,
    folio 10).
- **Legends & Miracles**
  - *One of the Five Pirs; he gives Ranjha a thick blanket.* "`Zalkaria` [text-layer?] [Zakaria] **a
    thick blanket**" (p. 11, folio 10, in the gift list quoted above).
  - *He shakes the earth and opens the way — the book's one unambiguous karamat by a named saint,
    told flat by the narrator with no hedge.* "And Ranjha prayed, 'May the Five `Pin` [text-layer?]
    [Pirs] bless my enterprise and make the way easy. **And `Pit` [text-layer?] [Pir] Bahauddin shook
    the earth, and the way was opened unto Ranjha, and a voice spoke, 'Jat, arise, go on your way. Why
    are you sleeping? The way has been opened for you.'**" (p. 50, folio 49).
- **Significance**
  - Listed among the Five Pirs' "spiritual celebrities" (p. 16, folio 15, note 10).
  - **Do not merge with the scriptural Zakaria.** Mithi's discourse on love names a different figure
    entirely — "Zakaria who got caught in a tree and was sawn asunder" (p. 18, folio 17) — and nothing
    in the book connects that one to Multan. See Doubts.
  - *Place-level.* This row is one of the archive's eight **Multan** rows; the book names Multan four
    times — see the Multan bullet below.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya` at p. 50, folio 49 rests on the single bare word "Bahauddin".**
  "`Pit` [text-layer?] [Pir] Bahauddin" appears there with no Zakariya, no Multan, no date and no
  order. It is the archive's only row whose principal figure carries that name, but **the name is
  common enough that a human must confirm the identification is intended**; if it is reversed, the
  earth-shaking karamat moves out of the row. (The row's other material, from pp. 10–11 and 16, is
  secure: the book glosses "Zakaria" as "Baha-ud-Din Zakaria of Multan".)

- **`shrine-of-fariduddin-ganjshakar`, `shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya`, `lal-shahbaz-qalandar`: no
  death year for any of them.** The rows carry 1266, 1267 and 1274; the book gives no date for any
  saint it names. **There is no death year, no AH date and no era date anywhere in the book for any
  archive figure** — the only dates in the whole volume are 1739, 1748, 1766, `i 768`, `i1761`, 1809,
  "1180 Hijri", "the year 120 of the Bikramajit era", the damaged Karbala pair `6' AN.` / `68o`, and
  the reign lengths "28 years" and "700 years".

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the time "When Shakarganj made his abode at Pakpattan", period not stated; translation of the poet's verse, the Invocation — FLAG: verse): "When Shakarganj made his abode at Pakpattan, the Punjab was delivered of all its troubles.4"; the annotator's note 4 rebuts it: "Much as one would like to agree with Waris Shah this is not historically correct. The Punjab has had plenty of troubles even after Baba Farid honoured it by making it his home and his place of burial!" (p. 2, folio 1).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: the annotator's present, "still"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Ranjha is still a well-known caste of the Jats in the old Punjab." (p. 3, folio 2).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: the annotator's present, "now"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Mauj Din is now a common name In the old Punjab." (p. 4, folio 3).
- (book's word: "the Punjab village"; refers to: past, "were", period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "Spinning parties — the Punjabi name is ‘tarinjan’ or ‘bhora’ — were a famous institution of the Punjab village. Young girls, generally unmarried ones, sat together wit [text-layer?] [with] their spinning-wheels and competed with each other in spinning skill and scandalous conversation." (p. 4, folio 3, note 14).
- (book's word: "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Lassi is a favourite drink in the Punjab countryside." (note 6); "Washing the hair with curds is still well known in the Punjab countryside." (note 8) (p. 4, folio 3).
- (book's word: "a Punjab village"; refers to: the annotator's present, "even now"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "This is how a morning begins even now in a Punjab village." — glossing the verse's dawn at the mosque (p. 5, folio 4, note 4).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Khojas are a well-known tribe of Hindu converts to Islam in the old Punjab who are mainly engaged in trade." (p. 7, folio 6, note 6).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the campaigns of Mahmood of Ghazna" and "1748" as printed; annotator's note, historical): "The Punjab has often been the battleground of invading forces from the North. The campaigns of Mahmood of Ghazna are famous. Here, the poet may be thinking of Ahead [text-layer?] [Ahmad] Shah Abdali, whose capital was Kandahar. and who, in 1748, began a series of invasions of India through the Punjab." (p. 8, folio 7, note 8); a later note on the verse "today the Punjab has fallen into the hands of Kandaharis5" says "This is probably a reference to the invasion of Ahmad Shah Abdali, whose capital was Kandahar. See Chapter 4, note 8." (p. 48, folio 47, note 5).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "in the past", and "the Anglo-Sikh treaty of 1809" as printed; annotator's note, historical): glossing the verse "as if the armies of the Punjab had fallen upon Hindustan.2" (p. 7, folio 6): "Historically speaking, this is an imaginary situation, as in the past the armies of the Punjab are known to have defended Hindustan on more than one occasion rather than to have fallen upon it at any time. Even the Sikh power which rose long after Waris Shah’s poem was written, was contained on the banks of the Sutlej river by the Anglo-Sikh treaty of 1809, , and never became a menace to Hindustan. The poet’s flight of fancy may have been inspired by a mistaken sense of local patriotism." (p. 8, folio 7, note 2). The book marks "the Sikh power" as a unit of its own; no boundary is inferred.
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; translation of the poet's verse — FLAG: verse similes, nothing said about the region beyond invasion as a figure of speech): "as if armies from Kandahar had swept over the Punjab.8" (p. 7, folio 6); "today the Punjab has fallen into the hands of Kandaharis5" (p. 47, folio 46); "looted him as armies have looted the Punjab3" (p. 44, folio 43 — the note answering marker 3 was not identified).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "There are also other ‘Bars’ in the old Punjab, such as ‘Nili Bar’ in the Montgomery area (now renamed Sahiwal). ‘Bar’ may he [text-layer?] [be] described as a jungle." (p. 10, folio 9, note 3; the note's first sentence, on Sandal Bar "between the Jhelum and Chenab flyers" [text-layer?] [rivers], is one territory and is not carried).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the more fashionable Punjabi turban"; refers to: "the late nineteenth century" as printed, and present tense; annotator's note, historical): the turban tuft "is the well-known ‘turrah’, beloved of the rural aristocracy of the Punjab. It spreads like a peacock’s tail over the side or the back of the more fashionable Punjabi turban. The tufted turban is a comparatively recent development, and i5 [text-layer?] [is] said to have been invented by the ‘mirasis’ or hereditary bards of the Punjab in the late nineteenth century." (p. 11, folio 10, note 7).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab area"; refers to: the annotator's present, "still"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): bhang "is a favourite with the mystically inclined, particularly among Muslims and Sikhs. It is still smoked, chewed, eaten or drunk in the old Punjab area by wandering mendicants and many of those seeking spiritual exaltation or sexual pleasure" (p. 12, folio 11, note 4).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; annotator's note, historical/literary): "Sohni-Mahiwal has its setting In The old Punjab." — Sohni "belonged to Gujurar [text-layer?] [Gujrat], on the river Chenab"; "SoJtni-Mahiwal [text-layer?] [Sohni-Mahiwal] and Sossi-Funoo [text-layer?] [Sassi-Punnoo] have also travelled beyond their original setting, being popular both in the old Punjab and Sind." (p. 12, folio 11, note 5). Also under `sindh`.
- (book's word: "the Punjab villages"; refers to: the annotator's present, "even now"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): of a mother's threat, "A grossly exaggerated way of asserting parental authority. The expression is by no means unfamiliar in the Punjab villages even now." (p. 14, folio 13, note 2).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Fingers in their mouths is a typical female gesture common in the old Punjab." (p. 14, folio 13, note 10).
- (book's word: "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: past, "had", period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "Old houses in the Punjab countryside had a big courtyard with a number of living-rooms and bedrooms round it. A number of brothers living with their parents as a joint family was quite common." (p. 16, folio 15, note 7).
- (book's word: "the rural Punjab"; refers to: past, "used to be", with "In our times" contrast; annotator's note, historical): "A spinning-wheel (charkha) used to be the inseparable companion of every woman, old or young, in the rural Punjab. Young girls and brides had red spinning-wheel, of smaller size. The spinning-wheel was an important link in rural society. Women spun cotton yarn, and the village weaver made it into cloth." (p. 17, folio 16, note 1).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab", "Punjab towns"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "The Gujars are a famous tribe of the old Punjab who keep animals and sell their milk. They also engage in agriculture on a large scale. A number of Punjab towns, such as Gujranwala, Gujrat and Gujar Khan are named after the Gujar tribe." (p. 19, folio 18, note 3).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: past, "was regarded", period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "Judging by their names, some of Hirs [text-layer?] [Hir's] girl friends are Hindus. - (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: present tense, "has always been", period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present — FLAG: about one tribe, framed against the region): "the Sial tribe has always been more than usually considerate towards its women. - (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: past, "were often worn", period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "A favorite game with young girls in the old Punjab. - (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, "is alive"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): of a ceremonial dish, "A more common dish for such purposes is rice cooked in ghee and well sprinkled with sugar. - (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; annotator's note, historical): "It may appear strange that a Muslim family should depend on Hindu Brahmans for fixing a marriage but this was by no means unusual in the old Punjab." (p. 26, folio 25, note 1).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Sawan and Bhadon are the two rainy months of the year in the old Punjab." (p. 26, folio 25, note 2).

- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: past, with "The social pattern is now changed"; annotator's note, historical): "Brides in the old Punjab wore coloured (and colourful) garments. - (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "A snub nose is not regarded as a point of beauty either in men or women in the old Punjab." (p. 39, folio 38, note 14).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: the annotator's present, "still"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "Jugglers and their bears are still a familiar sight in the old Punjab. Some have monkeys which are equally popular." (p. 40, folio 39, note 16).
- (book's word: "Punjabi mothers"; refers to: the annotator's present, "still"; annotator's note, ethnographic present — FLAG: an adjective of people, kept because it states a practice): "Lighting lamps in mosque even with ordinary oil is still a well-known way of expression of gratitude to God for His favours. Punjabi mothers still promise lamp-lighting in mosques when they pray for their children," (p. 41, folio 40, note 5).
- (book's word: "the old Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): the ‘Mehndhi’ is "a fringe of delicately intertwined strands of hair all round the forehead of a virgin girl in the old Punjab. The ‘Mehndhi’, which is a symbol of virginity, disappears at the wedding and is not resumed after marriage." (p. 43, folio 42, note 2).
- (book's word: "the Punjab countryside"; refers to: "even now"; annotator's note, ethnographic present): millet grain "is even now considered inferior food in the Punjab countryside." (p. 44, folio 43, note 7).
- (book's word: "the rural Punjab"; refers to: "is, or used to be"; annotator's note): "‘Dandasa’ is, or used to be, the equivalent of lipstick in the rural Punjab. It is made from the bark of certain trees which, when rubbed on the teeth and lips, gives them a gentle red colour." (p. 48, folio 47, note 2).
- (book's word: "the rural Punjab"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "While boys and girls in the rural Punjab move about freely to do their daily work and can see or talk to each other, it is not usual for them to do so if they are betrothed to be married. After the ‘mangni’ or betrothal, a girl does not see her fiancé until marriage" (p. 48, folio 47, note 6).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "Even now", against an earlier period not stated; annotator's note, ethnographic present): "The use of soap for shaving is a much later invention in the Punjab.
