- (book's word: "Punjabi homes"; refers to: present tense, period not stated; annotator's note — FLAG: adjective, kept because it states a domestic usage): the ‘pearha’ is "an article of furniture common in Punjabi homes." (p. 56, folio 55, note 5).
- (book's word: "the wretched Punjab"; refers to: the poet's own day as the Epilogue frames it — the poet dates the poem "in the year 1180 Hijri" on the same page; translation of the poet's verse, Epilogue, which Usborne says is "translated at full length" — FLAG: verse; the line turns at once to one town): "Of all the wretched Punjab I am most concerned for Kasur." (p. 56, folio 55). The preceding lament speaks of "the country" ("The country and the people have all been reduced to rump [text-layer?] [ruin?]. There is great disturbance throughout the country"), and the annotator's note 1 on it says "The latter half of the eighteenth century, to which Waris Shah is referring here, marked the decline of the Mughal Empire in India, and was a period of great confusion and disturbance" (p. 57, folio 56) — that note names India and the Mughal Empire, not Punjab.

## From Madho Lal Hussein, Verses of a Lowly Fakir (Alam tr.)

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/madho_lal_hussein_verses_lowly_fakir.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Place-level material: Multan

*The book's place-level material on "Multan" (8 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

  - Multan is named once in the verse, as the source of one item of a bride's trousseau: "The dress /
    someone carried across the snows / all the way from Kashmir. The dress / someone brought from
    Gujarat… The dress bought in Multan" (p. 51, folio 51, kafi 29).
  - The apparatus names it once more, in the same connection: "The red wedding dress, references to
    the various places of the Indian subcontinent (Kashmir, Gujarat, Multan) and then the image of
    Krishna with his flute—all add to the mystery." (p. 182, folio 182).
  - **That is the whole of it.** No Multan shrine, saint, ʿurs or custodian is named anywhere in the
    book: **(not stated)**.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: present day, "more than 400 years after his death", the book giving "Shah Hussein (1538–99)"; translator's argument): "Weaver, mystic, fakir, Shah Hussein continues to command great reverence in Punjab as a poet–saint more than 400 years after his death" (p. 6, folio 6).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "Shah Hussein’s times", the reign of Akbar — no year given; translator's historical argument): "there’s the relevant historical account of a rebellion brewing in Punjab led by Abdullah Bhatti, commonly known as ‘Dullah Bhatti, who was captured and hanged in public on Akbar’s orders" (the leading turned comma in "‘Dullah" is printed, not damage, per the takeaways Doubts) (p. 14, folio 14).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: Shah Hussein's time as dramatised; quoted source — Najm Hosain Syed's play *Takht L’hore (Throne of Lahore)*, a dramatic reconstruction, as reported by the translator): "Najm Hosain Syed extrapolates the socio-economic conditions of Punjab, not different from the ones cited in Moreland’s quote above" — Moreland's quoted account (W.H. Moreland, on "the so-called Golden Age of the Mughal rule") speaks of "India taken as a unit", not of Punjab, and is not itself a Punjab statement (p. 14, folio 14).
- (book's word: "Punjabis"; refers to: present day, "Even to this day"; translator's argument — FLAG: a people-word, and the book does not say which side of any border; kept because it describes the region's population): "Kafis, the stanzaic poems couched in folkloric themes and techniques, were easy to understand and memorize, and thus served as the ideal means to disseminate the mystical teachings among the illiterate masses dependent on the oral transmission of knowledge. Even to this day, most Punjabis and Sindhis are more likely to have heard a kafi than read one" (p. 15, folio 15).

## From Scott Kugle, Sufis and Saints' Bodies

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/kugle_sufis_and_saints_bodies.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the medieval centuries", relative to Kabir "a century earlier" — as the book states, no year given; author's argument, in an endnote): "New Muslims (naw-musalman) from weaver castes converted to join the Islamic community in Punjab slightly later than in Hindustan, where the mystic poet Kabir displays the same kind of Sufi-bhakti synthesis a century earlier. The castes of weavers who converted to Islam in the medieval centuries had most likely been Shudras" (ch. 4 n. 52, printed on p. 329, folio 308).
- (book's word: "Punjabi" — of the region's folk, poets and audiences, not of a language; refers to: Shah Hussayn's time and after, period not stated beyond that; author's argument): "the love epic of the Punjabi folk heroes Hir and her lover Ranjha"; "Punjabi poets and their eager audiences poured into Hir and Ranjha the same erotic emotion that was invested in Radha and Krishna in regions closer to the Ganges. The story of Hir and Ranjha became a favorite Sufi device for conveying the value of erotic passion in spiritual life"; Hussayn's poems explore divine love "in terms Islamic, Hindu, and indigenous Punjabi" (p. 228, folio 207). Borderline: kept because the sentence contrasts the region with "regions closer to the Ganges".
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the aftermath of "the uprising of 1857" — as the book states; book's historical prose): "After closely missing arrest, Imdadullah fled through the Punjab and Sindh, visiting Sufi shrines and eventually sailing from Karachi into exile in Mecca"; no shrine is named (p. 280, folio 259). An itinerary, not a description of the region: weigh lightly.

## From Richard M. Eaton, Essays on Islam and Indian History

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/eaton_essays_islam_indian_history.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The only row in this group with usable content. Named twice, as "Shaikh Baha' ad-Din Zakariya"
(p. 222) and as "Baha' al-Haqq Zakariya (d. 1263), whose tomb is in Multan city" (p. 227); the
chunk notes that the book uses both forms without flagging them as variants.

- His *wilayat* bordered Baba Farid's, and the border was described physically. Baba Farid told
  a traveller: "from here [Ajudhan] to such-and-such a village is in my charge, and from
  such-and-such a reservoir is the frontier of Shaikh Baha' ad-Din Zakariya [beyond which] is
  in his charge" (p. 222, n. 26, citing *Siyar al-arifin*, 115, which Eaton describes as "an
  early-sixteenth-century hagiography").
- He is named, with Baba Farid, as one of the two saints to whom "the vast majority" of
  medieval Jat and Rajput Muslim converts credit their conversion (p. 227).
- **Contradiction, kept:** the book gives d. 1263 (p. 227); the archive row gives 1267. The
  1263 date must not be imported over the archive's without a human check.

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, the present-day religious map; author's argument): "a glance at a map of South Asia's religious demography shows that Islam took root much more readily in Bengal or the Punjab than in upper India, the area with the deepest traditions of Indo-Muslim rule and patronage" (p. 14, folio 2).
- (book's word: "the western Punjab", "rural Punjab"; refers to: "between the thirteenth and nineteenth centuries", and the twentieth-century devotees of the 1938 case; author's argument, introduction summarising Chapters Nine and Ten): Chapter Nine "seeks connections between the processes of Islamization and peasantization in the western Punjab", asking "why and how it was that Jat tribesmen, as they abandoned pastoral nomadism for settled agriculture between the thirteenth and nineteenth centuries, gradually took on an Islamic identity" (p. 16, folio 4); Chapter Ten asks how the shrine "served to promote a conceptual conflation of Heaven and Earth among Muslims of rural Punjab" (p. 17, folio 5).
- (book's word: "Punjab and Sind"; refers to: "Beginning in the fourteenth century and continuing through the Mughal period (1526-1858)"; author's argument): "in both wings of India that became Muslim-majority regions—Bengal in the east, Punjab and Sind in the west—the growth of Muslim societies correlated with the adoption of sedentary agriculture. And both regions were still frontier societies where Hindu religious values and the hierarchic social ideals of Brahmin priests had not yet deeply penetrated"; "Thus in Punjab and Sind, shrines of great Sufis attracted and integrated pre-agrarian and non-Muslim pastoral clans into their ritual, socio-economic, and political orbits"; Sufi descendants married into the clan leadership, "the Delhi sultans and Mughal emperors granted huge tracts of rich land" for the shrines' ritual ceremonies, and the communities were "passing from a life of pastoral nomadism to one of settled wheat agriculture", helped by irrigation that "permitted a rapid growth in wheat production" (pp. 48–49, folios 36–37).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "by the eighteenth century"; author's argument): "by the eighteenth century large communities of Muslim peasants had appeared in both Punjab and Bengal. In contrast, in India's heartland … conversion to Islam was far less significant"; "Unlike Punjab or Bengal, Malabar lay beyond the orbit of Mughal imperial influence and the Persianized culture associated with it" (p. 49, folio 37).
- (book's word: "northwest Punjab"; refers to: 986; book's historical prose): "The pattern commenced in 986, when the Ghaznavid Sultan Sabuktigin (r. 977-97) attacked and defeated the Hindu Shahi raja who controlled the region between Kabul and northwest Punjab" (p. 110, folio 98).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "From the mid-eleventh century"; book's historical prose): Mahmud's successors "became progressively more provincial, their kingdom focused around their capital of Ghazni in eastern Afghanistan with extensions into the Punjab"; their raids "appear to have been less destructive and more sporadic than those of Sabuktigin and Mahmud" (p. 111, folio 99).
- (book's word: "Punjabi saints", "the Western Punjab"; refers to: 1911, which the author says is "equally true of medieval shrines"; quoted source, Major Aubrey O'Brien, "The Mohammedan Saints of the Western Punjab", 1911, in a footnote): "The general idea of our riverain folk seems to be that the Deity is a busy person, and that his hall of audience is of limited capacity … Thus, all human beings require an intervener between them and God" (p. 216, folio 204).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the late tenth century onward", culminating "in the thirteenth century"; book's historical prose): Mongol pressures "forced waves of Turkish settlers into the subcontinent, many of them settling permanently in the urban centres of the Punjab, such as Lahore, Dipalpur, and Multan"; Baba Farid's settling at Ajudhan was "the religious dimension of a very slow transformation of the Punjab's cities from a Hindu to a Turkish-Islamic orientation" (p. 217, folio 205).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "from the thirteenth century to the present", the essay's present; ethnographic present): writing ta'widh (amulets) for the masses "has not changed from the thirteenth century to the present; even now the successors of Baba Farid and his assistants, in common with those of other shrines in the Punjab, continue to write ta'widh for the masses" (p. 218, folio 206).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the mid-thirteenth century", Balban; book's historical prose): Balban, "in order to build a defensive bulwark against Mongol incursions, pursued the policy of strengthening and populating certain cities in the Punjab with large garrisons of Turkish elements", which "augment[ed] considerably the pattern of Muslim settlement in the urban centres of the Punjab, including not only the soldiers but also thousands of refugees fleeing before the advancing Mongols—artisans, merchants, petty officials, and the like"; the footnote names the garrisoned cities as "Lahore, Multan, Sirhind, Bhatinda, and Dipalpur" (pp. 222–223, folios 210–211).
- (book's word: "the western Punjab"; refers to: period of the essay's medieval argument, with "always" for the rainfall; book's historical prose, quoting Emily Hodges' dissertation in progress): the urban centres of the western Punjab were "economically based on intensive cultivation of food crops along the flood plains of the rivers"; beyond them "lay a vast tract of sparsely populated land, the barr country between the five rivers", "but little cultivated owing to the very scanty rainfall that has always-characterized [text-layer?] [always characterized] the western Punjab", supporting "pastoral nomadism based primarily on the herding of goats and camels"; the pastoralists "'moved only down to the riverain [sic] in the hot dry months and returned to the barr and ¢ha/ [text-layer?] [thal?] after rains, never leaving the Punjab plains and covering at the most a distance of less than one hundred miles'", in "a symbiotic relationship with the settled peoples of the riverine area" (p. 223, folio 211).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the eleventh century to Babur's "1519 and again in 1525"; book's historical prose): the Jat pastoralists, "before their entry into the Punjab, had not yet been integrated into Hindu society"; "By the time of Baba Farid, however, in the 1260s, we find the first mention of Jats occupying the Punjab proper" (the doabs named are listed under not attached); Babur "described Jat pastoralist groups in the Sind Sagar doab and Sialkot regions"; "The northward movement of Jat clans and their settlement in the grazing tracts of the Punjab is thus well supported by contemporary evidence" (pp. 224–225, folios 212–213).
- (book's word: "the Lahore and Multan sibas" [text-layer?] [subas], "Punjabi parganas", "the Punjab"; refers to: statistics "compiled about 1595", Abu al-Fazl's day, "by the 1650s", and "Babur's time"; book's historical prose, citing Abu al-Fazl, Ibbetson and Rose, and Irfan Habib): the sentence opens on damaged text, printed "i Akban ’statistics for the Lahore and Multan sibas, compiled about 1595" [text-layer?] [opening words lost]; counting castes named as Jat, "of the total 186 Punjabi parganas whose dominant zamindirs [text-layer?] [zamindars] are known, fully 82, or nearly half, were controlled by Jat groups"; "Only in the extreme western Punjab, in the Sind Sagar doab, where the Janjua Rajputs and Ghakkars dominated, were the Jats conspicuously absent as zamindars"; the Jats, the "'low Sudras' described by al-Birtni [text-layer?] [al-Biruni] in the eleventh century", had become "the dominant agrarian caste in nearly half of the Punjab"; "by the 1650s, as today, the very word 'Jat' had become virtually synonymous with peasant agriculturalist"; Habib's explanation is "the introduction of the Persian wheel in the Punjab and its extensive use by Babur's time", making "much of the arid western Punjab's naturally rich soil capable of supporting the cultivation of food crops". The scholar's name is printed "Abi al-Fadl" and "Abii al-Fadi" [text-layer?] [Abu al-Fadl] (p. 225, folio 213).
- (book's word: "Punjab", "Punjabi Muslim clans", "the rivers of the Punjab"; refers to: "the medieval period"; author's argument): the Jats' "migration north from Sind to Punjab, their rise in social status from low Sudras to zamindars, and their gradual transformation from pastoralists to farmers" was accompanied by conversion to Islam; "of those Jat and Rajput groups that became Muslim in the medieval period, the vast majority of them claim to have been converted either by Baba Farid or by his contemporary, Baha' al-Haqq Zakariya (d. 1263), whose tomb is in Multan city"; Table 9.1 lists "some of the Punjabi Muslim clans" that claim Baba Farid (Bhatti, Chhina, Dhudhi, Dogar, Gondal, Hans, Jo'iya, Khokhar, Siyal, Tiwana, Watt, Kharral, Ara'in); the earliest sources on Baba Farid "make no mention of his having converted anyone", and many of these clans, "in the course of their northward migrations up the rivers of the Punjab, had not yet reached the Ajudhan area during Baba Farid's lifetime" (p. 227, folio 215).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "during Jahangir's reign"; book's historical prose): the Jawahir-i Farid account was "written during Jahangir's reign, when the Mughals enjoyed effective authority in the Punjab"; "In the mid-eighteenth century, however, Mughal decline had allowed various local powers to assert their independence" (the decline sentence does not name the Punjab) (p. 231, folio 219).
- (book's word: "Punjabi clans"; refers to: "from the late fourteenth to the early nineteenth centuries", the span of the Siyal data; author's argument): from the Siyal genealogies of Jhang District (Table 9.2), "If these data on the Siyals are at all indicative of the conversion pattern for the other clans … then we may conclude that religious conversion among Punjabi clans was very slow indeed—not only slow, but probably unconscious as well", "a period long before either British census officials or zealous reformers began urging Indians to place themselves into sharply differentiated religious categories" (pp. 234–235, folios 222–223).
- (book's word: "the southwestern Punjab"; refers to: the 1935–38 court depositions; quoted source, the Lahore High Court record): "many of the major endogamous clans that are spread throughout the southwestern Punjab, mainly as peasant agriculturalists" claimed conversion by Baba Farid; "These clans, or binidaris [text-layer?] [biradaris] (brotherhoods), include the Wattu, the Siyal, the Dogar, the Kathia, the Bhatti, and the Tiwana" (p. 241, folio 229).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: Mughal officials newly arrived in Bengal, around 1620 in the surrounding narrative; book's historical prose): "The Bengali diet of fish and rice, for example, contrasted sharply with the wheat and meat diet of the Punjab and seems to have posed a special stumbling block for immigrants" (p. 263, folio 251).

## From Carl W. Ernst and Bruce B. Lawrence, Sufi Martyrs of Love

*Integrated 10 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/ernst_lawrence_sufi_martyrs_of_love.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

Seven bullets across three chunks. The book's forms are **"Shaykh Baha ad-Din"** (no nisba, p. 171,
folio 159), **"Shaykh Baha ad-Din Zakariyya"** (p. 184, folio 172) and `Baha' al-Din Zakariyya`
(p. 236, folio 226). **The book gives him no place, no death date, no tomb, no order and no Multan
of his own anywhere: all (not stated)** — the identification with the archive's Multan row rests on
the name, and in one chunk on a qawwal's Multan provenance; see Doubts.

- **Practices & Events — a qawwal's report of his samaʿ assembly and his hospice.** "One time a
  qawwal, Abu Bakr by name, came from Multan to visit Nizam ad-Din's teacher. \"I was reciting in
  sama' before Shaykh Baha ad-`Din;'[text-layer?]` [Din,\"] he said, \"and I reached this line: 'The
  snake of desire has stung my heart.' I could not recall the second half of the couplet, but the
  Shaykh did.\"" (p. 171, folio 159). "And then he began to extol the virtues of Shaykh Baha ad-Din,
  saying that in his hospice devotion to God was such that even the slave girls recited the names of
  God while grinding grain. In this fashion he rambled on, but his words did not make any lasting
  impression on the heart of the young Nizam ad-Din" (p. 171, folio 159).
- **Significance — he is the addressee of an extensive correspondence on whether shaykhs may hold
  wealth.** "He had extensive correspondence with Shaykh Baha ad-Din Zakariyya on the question of
  poverty and wealth." (p. 184, folio 172), of Hamid ad-Din Suwali Nagauri. The charge, in Hamid
  ad-Din's first letter: "`Accord-ing` to (the teaching of) the Shaykhs and the testimony of the
  `Qur' an` and hadith, the property of this world is supposed to be an obstacle to the attainment of
  union with God. Yet it has been heard that some of the Shaykhs of our time enjoy a handsome share
  of it, and at the same time perform miracles." (p. 184, folio 172). **The book does not say that
  Baha ad-Din Zakariyya is one of those shaykhs — the letter is addressed *to* him and the wealthy
  shaykhs are left unnamed: (not stated).** The second letter records that he did not answer, or
  answered unsatisfactorily: "This humble creature has sent a letter to Shaykh Baha ad-Din and
  requested him to resolve certain difficulties. For whatever reason your honor did not reply, or if
  you did reply, what you said was not satisfactory." (p. 184, folio 172); the resolution comes
  instead direct from God — "This humble man wept before God … God accepted my prayer, and His
  answer appeared in the following manner" (p. 184, folio 172).
- **He is a witness inside a Pakpattan text about Baba Farid** — the Chishtiyya-i bihishtiyya is
  described as `On Farid's postmortem miracles, khalifas, and testimony of Baha' al-Din Zakariyya.`
  (p. 236, folio 226). **He does not appear in the index at all.**

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **`shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya`** rests on the name form alone in chunks 013 and 017, and in
  chunk 012 on the name plus a qawwal's stated Multan provenance. The book gives him no place, no
  dates and no tomb.

### Place-level material: Multan

*The book's place-level material on "Multan" (8 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan"): **Multan is where Baba Farid pledged allegiance to the Chishti order, and the book characterises the city** — "The visit of Qutb ad-Din to Multan (a strategic commercial center in the Punjab) became the occasion for Farid ad- Din's pledge of allegiance to the Chishti master" (p. 80, folio 66). **No year: (not stated).**
- **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan"): **Multan was under Sikh attack in the early nineteenth century, and a Chishti successor is said to have resisted** — "Another successor of Nur Muhammad, Sulayman Taunsawi's contemporary Hafiz Muhammad Jamal (d. 1811), is also said to have taken part in resistance to Sikh attacks on Multan" (p. 121, folio 108).
- **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan"): **a qawwal came from Multan to Delhi, and reported the samaʿ practice of the Multani shaykh's hospice** — "One time a qawwal, Abu Bakr by name, came from Multan to visit Nizam ad-Din's teacher" (p. 171, folio 159).
- **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan"): **Multan is a place of publication and of manuscript holding in the book's bibliographies** — `The Teachings `ofKhwaja[text-layer?]` [of Khwaja] Farid. Multan, 1978.` (p. 225, folio 214), `Fifty Poems `ofKhawaja[text-layer?]` [of Khawaja] Farid. Multan, 1983.` (p. 230, folio 219), and `Ed. Multan. Ganj Bakhsh 22050/22022.` (p. 240, folio 230).

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated — "the early Muslim saints in general, and the Chishti masha'ikh in particular"; author's argument): "Not only for Gujarat, Bengal, but also for Uttar Pradesh, Punjab, and the Deccan does the significance of the early Muslim saints in general, and the Chishti masha'ikh in particular, mark a particular kind of South Asian spiritual authority that is not only political and religious but also cultural"; the authors describe "a spectrum of spiritually charged spaces or sites" that are "devotional loci for Indian Muslims from several classes, and also for Hindus" (p. 110, folio 97). Punjab is one of five regions named together; nothing specific to Punjab is said.
- (book's word: "the Punjab", which the book calls one of "two provinces"; refers to: Akbar's reign, the Fatehpur Sikri phase, which he "abandoned ... by 1585" — as the book states; author's argument): through Shaykh Salim and "Shaykh Farid ad-Din Ganj-i Shakar in the Punjab", "Through a twofold, redoubled Chishti loyalty, Akbar could anchor his imperial legitimacy in two provinces adjacent to Uttar Pradesh, the Punjab and Rajasthan, both of which also happened to be crucial to the political-military ambitions of his reign" (pp. 112–113, folios 99–100). The book's own unit is a Mughal-period "province"; its extent is not stated.
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the eighteenth century"; book's historical prose): "The Nizami Chishtis came to prominence in the eighteenth century, principally in Delhi, the Deccan, and Punjab, through masters who were related to but in tension with local political leaders" (p. 119, folio 106).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "Punjabi Hindus"; refers to: Shaykh Sulayman Taunsawi's time, "as British colonialism began to take hold of India" — Taunsawi is "(d. 1850)" on the same page; book's historical prose): Taunsawi "urged his followers to participate in resistance against Sikh aggression in the Punjab, and so, apparently for the first time in the annals of Chishti history, we have spiritual biographies drawn into partisanship on behalf of one political group (Muslim) over against another (non-Muslim)"; "Notable, however, is the effort of Shah Sulayman to maintain cordial relations with Punjabi Hindus even while he opposed their Sikh neighbors" (p. 121, folio 108). The supporting endnote (n. 10) cites M. Zameeruddin Siddiqi, "The Resurgence of the Chishti Silsilah in the Punjab during the Eighteenth Century" (p. 214, folio 203).
- (book's word: "the Punjab", "the southern Punjab"; refers to: Taunsawi "(d. 1850)", his successor Muhammad 'Aqil "(d. 1814)" and the latter's disciple Gul Muhammad Ahmadpuri "(d. 1827)" — as printed; book's historical prose): Taunsawi "consolidated the order by stressing learning through establishment of institutions: not only the academy called Dar al- 'Ulum at Taunsa on the upper Indus, but also other religious schools throughout the Punjab"; 'Aqil and Ahmadpuri "both of whom founded religious schools in the southern Punjab that provided free instruction and lodging to hundreds of students" (pp. 121–122, folios 108–109).
- (book's word: "the upper Punjab"; refers to: "the nineteenth century"; book's historical prose): the line from Shah Mirra "resurfaces in the nineteenth century in the figure of Hafiz Sayyid Muhammad 'Ali Khayrabadi, marking the expansion of the Nizami branch of Chishti Sufism through disciples of Shaykh Sulayman Taunsawi in the upper Punjab" (p. 71, folio 57).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: "the colonial period"; author's argument): "If we were to follow only the dictates of political history and the criteria of social science, the test case for the Chishti Nizami Sulaymaniyya lineage in the colonial period might seem to be the battles over shrines in the Punjab"; the example given is Taunsa, and "Political power struggles internal to the Sulaymaniyya can be traced to British manipulation and also the rivalry of local Sikhs, Hindus, and Muslim reformers" (p. 124, folio printed "Ill" [text-layer?] [111]). The authors then set this political reading aside: "political contestation is only part of the narrative".
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: present day ("continues to be intoned"); ethnographic present): "The name Qalandar continues to be intoned in popular qawwali lyrics of the Punjab, and it has been appropriated by a mainstream Sufi group, the Indian Qalandari order centered at Kakori near Lucknow" (p. 36, folio 22). Which side of the border is not stated.

## From Saiyid Athar Abbas Rizvi, A History of Sufism in India, vol. 1

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/rizvi_history_of_sufism_india_1.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

**42 bullets across chunks 008, 009, 010, 013, 014, 017, 018, 019, 024 and 026.** The book's names:
"Shaikh Baha’u'd-Din Zakariyya of the Suhrawardi si/sila [OCR?] [silsila] in Multan" (p. 146, folio 128),
"Shaikh Baha’u’d-Din Zakariyya Suhrawardi" (p. 147, folio 129), "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya'" (p. 173,
folio 155), and "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din" throughout. **The book's death date disagrees with the archive row's
by five years and the disagreement is recorded, not reconciled.**

- **Saint**
  - **Death: the book says December 1262, twice and consistently; the archive row says 1267.** "Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din died on 7 Safar, 661/21 December, 1262" (p. 212, folio 194), sourced to "AA, p. 28";
    "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya died in December 1262, ten years earlier" (p. 226, folio 206) — given
    incidentally, inside a chronological argument about Amir Husain. The index prints the same:
    "Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya, Shaikh (b. 1182-83, d. 1262), 128-29, 134, 155, 162, 191, 198-200, 203-6, 210,
    214 17, 220-22, 277, 303, 306, 389" (p. 461, folio 437) — **"214 17" is printed without its dash, and
    the entry is physically split by the page furniture, the head standing above the `+ Google` artifact and
    the dates below it.** **The index also supplies a birth date the archive row lacks: b. 1182-83.**
  - **Chunks that print no date for him at all**, so that the disagreement does not recur there: pp. 146–147
    (folios 128–129), p. 153 (folio 135), p. 173 (folio 155), pp. 233–248 (folios 213–228), p. 292
    (folio 272), p. 299 (folio 277), pp. 325–328 (folios 303–306), p. 413 (folio 389) — all (not stated).
  - **He was rich, and the book says so in its own voice.** "This remark was made in reference to Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din, himself extremely rich" (p. 146, folio 128, footnote 6); he was "mystically prompted by
    the teachings of his pir, who also had vast wealth" (p. 147, folio 129). **His pir is not named there —
    (not stated).**
  - **The hinge of the order's whole Indian history, as an exclusive claim.** The other disciples of
    Shihabu'd-Din Suhrawardi "were eminent in their own right, but the continuous history of the silsila can
    be traced only through the khalifas of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya" (p. 220, folio 202).
  - **His relations with a rival, recorded in both directions.** He travelled with Shaikh Jalalu'd-Din
    Tabrizi from Baghdad to Nishapur and "would constantly pray and meditate" while the other "would visit
    local dervishes and spend time sightseeing"; they parted at Nishapur (p. 217, folio 199). At Delhi he was
    made chairman of the mahzar precisely because of "the rivalry between Shaikh Jalalu'd-Din and Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya" (p. 218, folio 200) — and then destroyed the proceeding by deference: he "ran to
    receive him and carry his shoes", saying that Jalalu'd-Din "had served his pir for seven years and
    therefore it was fitting for him to use the dust from Shaikh Jalalu'd-Din's feet as an eye-wash" (p. 218,
    folio 200).
  - **Sparing with praise, with one exception.** "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya, not well-known for his
    praise of other sufis, was a great admirer of Shaikh Ahmad" — the weaver Shaikh Ahmad Naharwani (p. 216,
    folio 198).
  - **A friendship with the Chishti leader at Multan.** Khwaja Qutbu'd-Din Bakhtiyar Kaki, leaving Baghdad,
    "travelled through Khurasan to reach Multan. There he established a friendship with Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din
    Zakariyya" (p. 153, folio 135).

- **Shrine / Darbar, khanqah and economy**
  - **The economy of his khanqah, stated twice and consistently.** "The prosperity of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din
    Zakariyya's khanqah depended mainly on gifts from merchants and trade guilds, for even local governors
    were often short of funds. As many merchants traded outside India, to a large extent, they were
    independent of the government and could not be prevented from giving futuh" (p. 242, folio 222); and of
    the Suhrawardis generally, "They accepted state patronage, received huge gifts from merchants and artisan
    guilds, and treasured riches as future assets" (p. 236, folio 216).
  - **The end of that prosperity, dated by an event and not by a year.** "This marked the end of the
    prosperity of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya's khanqah in Multan. The Suhrawardi silsila, however, started
    flourishing in other areas, while Multan became a mere shadow of its former glory" (p. 235, folio 215) —
    **the referent of "This" is the execution of Shaikh Hud and the seizure of the khanqah property (p. 234,
    folio 214).**
  - **Hospitality rationed and charity fixed by him personally.** "The food in Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din
    Zakariyya's khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] was abundant, but was offered to only a select few. Often the Shaikh
    ordered his servants to pay fixed amounts in charity to those he considered deserving and only the sum he
    specified was paid" (p. 236, folio 216).
  - **His khanqah described at second hand, and the one detail is about his slave girls.** Nizamu'd-Din, at
    twelve, "first heard of Baba Farid through a qawwal (a musician) who had been to both Multan and Ajodhan.
    The gawwal [OCR?] [qawwal] first related to Nizamu'd-Din the intensely religious atmosphere in the
    khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya', where even his slave girls performed constant
    zikr" (p. 173, folio 155). **The book does not there say where that khanqah was; see Doubts.**
  - **His khanqah took in a wandering qalandar party and kept one of them.** The qalandars "reached Multan
    where they stayed with Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din"; when the party dispersed in a storm "'Iraqi returned to the
    Shaikh's khangah [OCR?] [khanqah]" and "began living in a cell which the Shaikh had assigned him, as a
    spiritual retreat" (p. 224, folio 204).
  - **The estate he left, and the number of heirs.** "Shaikh Sadru'd-Din had six brothers, but he alone
    inherited from his father property worth 700,000 tankas. According to Jamali, he immediately gave the
    entire amount to the poor, for like his father he believed he was unable to spend it judiciously"
    (p. 220, folio 202). **A rival account stands on the same page — see Doubts.** Rizvi's conclusion: "it
    seems clear that wealth accumulated by Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din did not long remain with his sons" (p. 220,
    folio 202).
  - **His descendants as a political grievance after his time** — Shaikh Hud's alleged war-cry was "the
    ill-treatment of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya's descendants by the Sultan" (p. 234, folio 214).

- **Practices & Events**
  - **He barred prostration before himself and required prayers first — directly relevant to what is done at
    his shrine.** "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya did not encourage his disciples to perform sajda. They
    greeted him with: as-salam alaikum (Peace be with you). He also expected his disciples to finish their
    obligatory religious duties first and to greet him afterwards" (p. 240, folio 220).
  - **The negative on faith-cures, and it is the author's inference.** "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya was
    concerned only with the élite and it is unlikely that people visited his khangah [OCR?] [khanqah] for
    faith cures. By contrast, the Chishti jama'at-khanas were filled with people wanting amulets" (p. 240,
    folio 220). **Note the hedge "it is unlikely"; Rizvi cites no source.**
  - **His food doctrine, quoted from the Qurʾan.** "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya was a lively host and
    followed literally the Qur'anic injunction: 'eat of what is pure and act righteously.'" (p. 237,
    folio 217).
  - **He refused qalandars — and broke the rule twice.** "Qalandars were intolerable to Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din"
    (p. 236, folio 216); "As also mentioned previously, Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din disliked qalandars intensely and
    certainly never encouraged their unwelcome visits." (p. 328, folio 306) — yet "Among the Suhrawardiyyas,
    Shaikh Fakhru'd-Din 'Iraqi was a qalandar and as described earlier, even after his initiation by Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya he continued to live as a wandering dervish" (p. 328, folio 306), and he adopted
    Lal Shahbaz as his disciple and named him (p. 328, folio 306). **The book flags the two as exceptions and
    still states the dislike absolutely — see Doubts.**
  - **His position on poetry and music, and the exception he made.** "Baha'u'd-Din was opposed to poetry and
    music, but he tolerated its indulgence by Fakhru'd-Din Ibrahim" (p. 224, folio 204). His other disciples
    "were shocked to learn that this ghazal was being sung in local taverns to the accompaniment of the harp
    and zither. They complained to the Shaikh" (p. 224, folio 204) — and he ruled for 'Iraqi.
  - **He insisted on single initiation, and the rule broke after him.** "Although Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din
    Zakariyya had insisted during his lifetime that sufis join one order only, from the fourteenth century
    sufis were often initiated into both the Chishtiyya and the Suhrawardiyya" (p. 292, folio 272).
  - **Multan as his seat and as the qalandars' route into India.** "Mongol domination of Central Asia and Iran
    facilitated the movement of the Qalandariyyas and the Jawaliqi groups from Turkey and Egypt to India.
    Passing through Multan, they often came into conflict with Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya" (p. 325,
    folio 303), "but such experiences were counteracted by the generosity of Baba Farid" (p. 325, folio 303).

- **The dispute about whether a sufi may hold wealth — the fullest treatment of shrine wealth in the book**
  - "In order to ascertain the attitude of the Suhrawardi order towards wealth, Shaikh Hamidu’d-Din wrote a
    number of letters to Shaikh Baha’u'd-Din Zakariyya… Although the latter replied, Shaikh Hamidu'd-Din was
    not completely satisfied with the answers received" (p. 146, folio 128). The two met at Delhi "during the
    mahzar4 to resolve the charges against Shaikh Jalalu’d-Din Tabrizi" (p. 146, folio 128), *mahzar* glossed
    as "An assembly of ‘ulama’ and sufis to decide an important dispute; literally the word means either a
    royal court or any document attested by witnesses" (p. 146, folio 128, footnote 4). **No date for the
    mahzar — (not stated).**
  - **His defence, in his own reported words.** "Although wealth is a serpent, someone who has learnt the
    incantation to overcome the venom, need not have any fear from the serpent"; and, pressed, "Your
    achievement as a dervish is not endowed by such beauty and elegance as to be immune from the evil eye, but
    our achievements as dervishes are endowed with such great beauty as to gain no harm even if the whole of
    the world’s blackness were applied to its face" (pp. 146–147, folios 128–129). **The book twice records
    that he lost the exchange:** "Shaikh Baha’u’d-Din was speechless" and "Shaikh Baha’u’d-Din Zakariyya
    Suhrawardi was unable to reply" (p. 147, folio 129), sourced "Sururu's-Sudur, pp. 86-7".
  - **A son carried the quarrel to Ajmer.** "Controversy over the acquisition of material wealth by members of
    the Suhrawardi order annoyed one of Shaikh Baha’u’d-Din’s sons, who visited Ajmer. Discovering that Shaikh
    Hamidu’d-Din did not attend Friday congregational prayers, he used this as a pretext to attack the Shaikh
    and gained some support from a group of ‘ulama’ who demanded that the Shaikh fulfil his religious duties"
    (p. 147, folio 129). **The son is not named — (not stated).**

- **Succession and disciples**
  - **Hereditary succession, arranged by himself.** "He appointed his own son, Sadru'd-Din, known as 'Arif
    (Gnostic), his khalifa" (p. 220, folio 202).
  - **ʿIraqi's deputisation, in four instruments and in order.** He "declared 'Iraqi's training to be
    complete. He ordered him to give up retirement and meditation, presented him with his own khirga [OCR?]
    [khirqa] to wear and later gave him his daughter in marriage. Before he died, Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din
    appointed him his khalifa" (pp. 224–225, folios 204–205). **Robe, marriage into the family, and the
    khalifa appointment — no document of any kind is mentioned (not stated).** And the succession broke at
    once: "after his death, the jealousy of other disciples forced 'Iraqi to leave Multan about 1263. It would
    appear he had been there since about 1239" (p. 225, folio 205) — **a twenty-four-year residence given as
    two approximations.**
  - **Rizvi names the poet as the chief reason for his scholarly fame.** "The main reason for Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya's fame in the scholarly world, however, was due to the achievements of his
    disciple, Shaikh Fakhru'd-Din Ibrahim, popularly known as 'Iraqi" (p. 224, folio 204). A devotional verse
    of ʿIraqi's names him as the guide of the world: "'If thou shouldst ask of the world 'Who is the guide of
    men?' / Thou wilt hear from heaven no other answer than 'Zakariyya'.'" (p. 225, folio 205); and in the
    Kanzu'r-Rumuz "Husaini praised the Shaikhs, Shihabu'd-Din Suhrawardi, Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya and
    Sadru'd-Din" (p. 226, folio 206).
  - **A named disciple who is himself an archive row.** "Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya had many famous
    disciples, one was Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din Bukhari, also called Jalal Surkh (Red)" (p. 223, folio 203) — see
    `shrine-of-jalaluddin-surkh-posh-bukhari-jalaluddin-bukhari` below. He is also named in one clause as the
    pir behind the Uch line: "a disciple of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya" (p. 299, folio 277).
  - **His illiterate disciple, and the boast attached to him.** "One of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din's disciples, Hasan
    Afghan, was illiterate. The Shaikh was proud of Hasan Afghan and used to say that if God asked him what he
    had brought from the world, he would present Hasan as a gift" (p. 223, folio 203).
  - **Two authorities attach Amir Husain to him and Rizvi refutes both on chronology.** "Jamali describes him
    in connection with the disciples of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya"; "Modern scholars also describe him as
    Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din's disciple" (p. 226, folio 206); Husain "must have been born about 671/1272-73, while
    Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya died in December 1262, ten years earlier" (p. 226, folio 206).

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **A prenatal prophecy about his own grandson, and an endorsement against the child's father.** He
    "prophesied his prominence while he was still in his mother's womb" (p. 230, folio 210); and "at the age of
    four, Ruknu'd-Din donned Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din's turban, and although his father objected strongly, Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din approved of the child's action" (p. 231, folio 211). **His turban then becomes succession
    regalia two generations on: "After Shaikh Ruknu'd-Din succeeded his father, he wore the same turban and
    Shaikh Sadru'd-Din's khirga [OCR?] [khirqa]" (p. 231, folio 211).**
  - **A frontier of spiritual authority shared with Baba Farid** — beyond a certain water tank "began the area
    of Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din whose prayers he should attain" (p. 211, folio 193).
  - **Guru Nanak's reported discussion with him at Multan, which the book reads as a conversation with the
    dead.** "At Ajodhan and Multan, Guru Nanak was reported to have had discussions with Baba Farid and Shaikh
    Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya… The suggestion is far-fetched although Guru Nanak did undoubtedly enjoy the
    company of sufis in these towns" (p. 413, folio 389).

- **Significance**
  - **The book's Multan is his Multan**: the Suhrawardi seat, the qalandars' obstacle, the merchant-funded
    khanqah, and the point from which the order's Indian history is traced (p. 220, folio 202). **It gives the
    shrine nothing: no tomb, no fabric, no ʿurs, no custodian, no endowment — all (not stated).**

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

| row | the book prints | the archive row | status |
|---|---|---|---|
| `shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya` | **7 Safar, 661/21 December, 1262** (p. 212, folio 194) and "died in December 1262" (p. 226, folio 206); index "d. 1262" (p. 461, folio 437) | `figure_died` **1267** | **A five-year gap, too large for a calendar-conversion rounding, and the printed forms carry no OCR damage. Must be checked before either date enters an entry.** |

4. **`shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya` has four single-clause bullets** — p. 153 (folio 135), p. 292
   (folio 272), p. 299 (folio 277), p. 413 (folio 389) — **each a bare name with no date, no place and no
   act, and none of them shrine evidence.**

### Place-level material: MULTAN

*The book's place-level material on "MULTAN" (8 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **prahladpuri-temple, shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya, shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani, shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi, shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam, shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari, shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez, shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** — **MULTAN, the archive's 8 rows, and the most substantive place-level material in the book.** "Multan had become a dependency of the Fatimids of Egypt"; Mahmud's blow to Ismaʿili power there; "in 1175 Mu‘izzu'd-Din Muhammad bin Sam seized Multan"; "by the middle of the eleventh century, sufism had penetrated into the areas surrounding Multan" (p. 128, folio 110); "the news of Sultan Mahmud's conquest of Multan" (p. 129, folio 111). Later: the ʿulamaʾ of Multan refusing a fatwa (pp. 158–159, folios 140–141); a Malik "of Uch and Multan" (p. 172, folio 154); Qutbuʾd-Din Kashani's madrasa, "the Governor of Multan", "the Qazi of Multan", "the Multan fort" and the townfolk (pp. 209–210, folios 191–192); "governor of Multan" and "Maulana Husamu'd-Din of Multan" (pp. 187, 197; folios 169, 179); two bare mentions at pp. 285 and 287 (folios 265, 267); "he went to Multan and Uch" (p. 337, folio 315); "Seizing Multan and Delhi" (p. 335, folio 313); "visited Ajodhan and Multan" (p. 366, folio 344); "(at Delhi from Uch, near Multan)" (p. 258, folio 238); and the index headword "Multan, … 179--80, … 203 5, …" (p. 477, folio 453), **its number string damaged and not repaired.** **Two Multan mentions in pp. 1–29 are recorded by chunk 001 without page references and are converted at chunk level.** **NOT converted: "the Multani dialect" (pp. 350–351, folios 328–329), which is a language, and the nisba "Taju'd-Din Multani" (p. 240, folio 220), which is a private individual.**

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Ghaznawid rule, "Yamain al-Dawla Mahmud (388/998-421/1030)" and "Bahram Shah (1118-52)" as printed; book's historical prose): Mahmud "established Ghaznawid rule over Khurasan, Afghanistan and the Panjab"; after two defeats by 'Ala'u'd-Din of Ghur, "Bahram Shah was forced to flee to the Panjab" (p. 83, folio 67).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: Mahmud of Ghazna's annexation and after, with Hujwiri reaching Lahore "in c. 1035, while Sultan Mas'ud I (1031-41) reigned in Ghazna"; book's historical prose): "the annexation of the Panjab by Mahmud of Ghazna and its incorporation into his empire prompted many sufis to settle in the area"; Khattali "ordered his disciple, Shaikh Husain Zinjani to move to the Panjab", and later sent Hujwiri after him (p. 130, folio 112).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "the middle of the twelfth century", after "'Ala'u'd-Din Husain of Ghur in 1150-51, followed by the Ghuzz invasion six years later"; book's historical prose): Baba Farid's ancestors "came from Kabul, but migrated to the Panjab in the middle of the twelfth century" (p. 156, folio 138); the destruction of Ghazna and the Ghuzz invasion "drove a large number of people from that region to the Panjab for safety"; the book names "The Ghaznawid Sultan of the Panjab" as the one who made Farid's father Qazi of Kahtwal (p. 157, folio 139).
- (book's word: "the southern part of the Panjab"; refers to: "1175", as printed; book's historical prose): "A hundred and fifty years later in 1175 Mu'izzu'd-Din Muhammad bin Sam seized Multan, establishing firm control and Sunni rule over the southern part of the Panjab" (p. 128, folio 110).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: after Jalalu'd-Din's defeat "in November 1221" and Chingiz Khan's march back west; book's historical prose): Chingiz Khan's "absence enabled Itutmish [OCR?] [Iltutmish] to consolidate power in the Panjab, seven years later routing from Sind his rival, Nasiru'd-Din Qubacha" (p. 152, folio 134).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "previous years" before Muhammad bin Tughluq's scheme "attempted in 1327"; book's historical prose): the Sultan raised "the slogan of jihad against the Mongols, who in previous years had devastated the Panjab and the region around Delhi" (p. 193, folio 175).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "Timur's invasion in 1398-99", as printed; book's historical prose): "Timur's invasion in 1398-99 devastated both the Panjab and Delhi" (p. 290, folio 270).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: the time of Saiyid Jalalu'd-Din Bukhari, Makhdum-i Jahaniyan, born "15 Sha'ban 707/9 February 1308", and of "Sultan Firuz Shah Tughlug" [OCR?] [Tughluq]; book's historical prose): the Suhrawardiyya order "spread from Uch to Gujarat, the Panjab and Kashmir even extending its influence to the Delhi of Sultan Firuz Shah Tughlug [OCR?] [Tughluq]" (p. 299, folio 277).
- (book's word: "the Punjab"; refers to: the time of "Mongol domination of Central Asia and Iran", Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya and Baba Farid; book's historical prose): Qalandariyyas and Jawaliqis passed through Multan, where they "often came into conflict with Shaikh Baha'u'd-Din Zakariyya"; "From the Punjab, the displeased qalandars moved to Delhi creating scenes wherever they saw a khanqah. Some went as far east as Bengal" (p. 325, folio 303).
- (book's word: "the Panjab"; refers to: "the fifteenth century", with "The Mongol invasions between the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries" behind it; book's historical prose): "In the fifteenth century, the Panjab enjoyed a peaceful period due to Afghan rule. The Mongol invasions between the thirteenth and fourteenth centuries had devastated portions of the Panjab and the Afghans had successfully contributed to its re-settlement and urbanization by founding many new towns and promoting trade and commerce" (p. 408, folio 384).
- (book's word: "the Panjab", "west Panjab"; refers to: "About 1520" and Babur's third invasion "in 926/1520", as printed; book's historical prose): Guru Nanak returned from eastern India "only to find the Panjab plunged into a number of significant political crises"; Babur "started unsuccessful negotiations with Ibrahim to annex west Panjab to Kabul. After two minor raids around the region between the Indus and Jhelum, Babur invaded a third time over the Panjab in 926/1520 reaching as far as Sialkot"; a footnote says Daulat Khan "Gradually he also became the governor of the Panjab and in [1523 [OCR?] invited Babur to invade India" (p. 409, folio 385).
- (book's word: "Panjabi", "the Panjab"; refers to: "Guru Nanak's period"; quoted source, W.H. McLeod, *Guru Nanak and the Sikh religion*, quoted so that Rizvi can reject it): "the Panjabi sufism of Guru Nanak's period had evidently departed radically from the classical pattern of Arab and Persian Sufism"; most Muslims Nanak met "would, however, represent in varying degrees the blend of modified orthodoxy and debased Sufism which was dominant … in the Muslim community of the Panjab during this period" (pp. 419–420, folios 395–396).
- (book's word: "Panjabis", "the Panjab"; refers to: "the time of Guru Nanak"; author's argument, against McLeod): "all literate Panjabis, both Hindus and Muslims, at the time of Guru Nanak were conversant with" 'Attar, Rumi and Sa'di; McLeod "seems to be unaware of Shaikh 'Abdu'l-Quddus Gangohi who lived in Shahabad, his disciple, Shaikh Jalal of Thaneswar, and the Qadiris who had made the Panjab the main centre of their activities" (p. 420, folio 396).

## From Omar Kasmani, Queer Companions

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/kasmani_queer_companions.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

*The book prints "Bahaʾuddin Zakaria". Also inside the Multan line below.*

- **He initiated Lal, in one tradition.** "According to the Suhrawardi Sufi tradition, the saint was
  initiated in Multan by Bahaʾuddin Zakaria (Boivin 2011, 18)." (p. 184, folio 167, intro n. 34)
- **One of the "four friends".** "The other three figures are Bahaʾuddin Zakaria (d. 1267) of Multan,
  `Faridud-din[text-layer?]` [Fariduddin] Ganjshakr (d. 1266) of `Pak-­Pattan`, and Jalaluddin
  Surkhposh Bukhari (d. 1291) of Uchh. Their shrines are spread across southern Punjab in Pakistan.
  Chothanbi, a revered site between `Lal-­bagh` and Sehwan that pilgrims visit, is believed to be
  their place of meeting." (p. 188, folio 171, ch. 1 n. 17) The group is named elsewhere as "a quartet
  of mystics, chahar-yar or 'four friends'" (p. 62, folio 45) and indexed as `four friends (mystics),
  45, 171n17` (p. 220, folio 203), **but the note itself gives the group no name: (not stated)**.
- **The death year 1267 and the city are the book's own words and match the archive row.** Nothing
  else about the shrine is given: **(not stated)**.

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **Name-spelling divergences between book and archive, all of which a consolidator will hit:** book
  "Bahaʾuddin Zakaria" vs archive "Bahauddin Zakariya"; book "`Faridud-din` Ganjshakr" vs archive
  "Baba Fariduddin Mas'ud Ganjshakar"; book "`Pak-­Pattan`" vs archive "Pakpattan"; book "Jalaluddin
  Surkhposh Bukhari" vs archive "Sayyid Jalaluddin Surkh-Posh Bukhari"; book "Uchh" vs archive "Uch
  Sharif"; book "Madho Lal Husain" vs archive "Madho Lal Hussain"; book "Bibiyan-pak-daman" vs
  archive "Bibi Pak Daman"; book "Data Ganj Bukhsh" and "Data Ganj Bakhsh" four years apart. **The
  death years 1267, 1266 and 1291 match the archive exactly, which is what makes those three
  identifications safe.**

### Place-level material: Multan

*The book's place-level material on "Multan" (8 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

  - **Multan was Lal's first destination.** "A few sacred sites in Balochistan and Sindh are the only
    traces that help picture the route his journey might have taken to Multan where he was first
    headed" (p. 62, folio 45); "he is likely to have first arrived in Multan in 1263" (p. 188,
    folio 171, ch. 1 n. 14).
  - **An ecstatic scene at Multan.** "sources mention a rare scene in Multan, whereby Lal is found in
    an ecstatic performance in the company of other Sufi fig-ures (Boivin 2011, 18)" (pp. 62–63,
    folios 45–46). **The other Sufi figures are not named: (not stated).**
  - **Multan as the place of Lal's initiation, in one tradition.** "According to the Suhrawardi Sufi
    tradition, the saint was initiated in Multan by Bahaʾuddin Zakaria (Boivin 2011, 18)." (p. 184,
    folio 167, intro n. 34) "Bahaʾuddin Zakaria (d. 1267) of Multan… Their shrines are spread across
    southern Punjab in Pakistan." (p. 188, folio 171, ch. 1 n. 17)

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "central Punjab"; refers to: "the colonial period" set against "the postcolonial state", as the book reports them; quoted source, Umber bin Ibad 2019): "Though focusing only on central Punjab, Ibad notes that in the colonial period, “the state did not try to control shrines directly through its bureaucracy”; however, the postcolonial state has gone further, especially by virtue of implementing a unique politico-religious ideology" — in his view, a desire "to harness the pluralistic traditions of shrines in favor of “representing a Singular Islam”" (Ibad 2019, introduction) (p. 69, folio 52).
- (book's word: "central Punjab"; refers to: stated in the present tense as a general condition, framed by Baba's childhood in a Punjab village "Decades on" from the 2012–2013 tellings; period otherwise not stated; author's argument): "Being a child in a poor rural family in central Punjab, one who is regarded as intersex means being given to particular bodily horizons, oriented to certain futures more than others"; "a most likely trajectory for a child such as Baba would have eventually involved a life with the khwaja-sara"; such children, "Given male names at birth and in most cases also registered as sons", "do not extend the social promise of family lines that cisgender sons are expected to offer" (p. 73, folio 56).
- (book's word: "parts of Sindh and Punjab"; "Rural Punjab and Sindh" in the cited title; refers to: period not stated — the study is dated 2010 in the References; quoted source, Lubna Chaudhry): "In a study of women and poverty in parts of Sindh and Punjab, Lubna Chaudhry notes how unmarried girls in their teens and early twenties are most vulnerable. She points to discriminatory food portions within the family and limited access to health care, in addition to a lack of share in economic resources, even those that involve their labor" (p. 191, folio 174, ch. 2 n. 24; the study is "Women and Poverty: Salient Findings from a Gendered Analysis of a Quasi-Anthropological Study in Rural Punjab and Sindh", 2010, p. 205, folio 188).

## From Michel Boivin, Hindu Sufis of South Asia

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/boivin_hindu_sufis_south_asia.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

The book's names for him: **"Bahauddin Zakariyya"**, **"Baha al-Din Zakariyya"**, **"Baha al-Din"**,
and **"the famous Sufi of Multan"**. Present in chunks 003, 009, 010 and 013.

- **Saint**
  - **Death year as printed, "(d. 1267)"** (p. 187, folio 174) — **which agrees with the archive row.
    No birth year: (not stated).** Chunks 003 and 009 give him no dates at all.
  - **Order, printed "Sohrawardi" / "Sohrawardiyya", not the archive's "Suhrawardi"**: "a famous Sufi
    Master of the **Sohrawardiyya**" (p. 51, folio 38); "initiated to the **Sohrawardi** tariqa by Baha
    al-Din Zakariyya" (p. 178, folio 165); "both Sufis were members of the Sohrawardi order, and that
    **Shah Rukn-e Alam was none other than the grandson and successor of Baha al-Din Zakariyya**."
    (p. 187, folio 174) **This is the book's only statement of his lineage.**
  - **Named as the initiator of another saint, in four passages**: "they recognize that Pithoro Pir was a
    Muslim Sufi initiated by Bahauddin Zakariyya" (p. 51, folio 38); "a tazkira refers to him as a
    disciple of the same Bahauddin Zakariyya already mentioned" (p. 51, folio 38) — of Udero Lal; Pithoro
    Pir "was initiated to the Sohrawardi tariqa by Baha al-Din Zakariyya. Thus, nobody challenges the
    fact that the saint was a Sufi." (p. 178, folio 165); and twice more in the summing-up, both hedged:
    "there is only one concrete link with Sufism: Pithoro Pir **would have been** initiated to Sufism by
    Baha al-Din Zakariyya. Such an element is incorporated into an oral tradition, and it can be related
    to a kind of legitimization of Pithoro's charisma." (p. 192, folio 179); "Pithoro Pir **is said to
    have been** initiated onto the Sohrawardi path by Baha al-Din Zakariyya." (p. 194, folio 181)

- **Shrine / Darbar**
  - **No shrine, no tomb, no building date and no toponym beyond the epithet "of Multan" is attached to
    him anywhere in the book: (not stated). The archive row's `Multan City` must not be back-filled from
    the row.**
  - **Index**: "Zakariyya, Baha al-Din 38, 165, 175, 179, 181" (p. 252, folio 239). **Five folios and
    nothing else: no dates, no place, no shrine, no silsila — (not stated).** His folio 175 is also the
    sole folio of chunk 012's "Alam, Shah Rukn-e 175" (p. 239, folio not stated); **the index does not
    connect the two entries: (not stated).** His folio 38 is the index's densest single folio in
    chunk 013, shared with "Tahir, Shaykh 38, 165", "Sitala (smallpox goddess) 38", "Sufism 4–7, 15, 36,
    38", "Sufism — and Bhagti 38", "Sohrawardiyya 38, 43, 85, 126, 187, 204 n.1", "tazkiras … 8, 38",
    "Udero Lal 29–30, 38, 41, 165" and "worship, categories of 37–8" (pp. 250–252, folios 237–239).

- **Legends & Miracles**
  - **The founding miracle of a Dalit-venerated saint, worked by him while touring in Sindh**: "One Madan
    had asked for the intercession of Baha al-Din Zakariyya (d. 1267) to obtain a son, when the famous
    Sufi of Multan was touring in Sindh. Baha al-Din offered him a small end (**pithu**) of a dried date.
    At the moment his wife tasted the blessed piece, she became pregnant. The son whom they had was named
    Pithoro, or the name of the date end. Thereafter, Pithoro became the disciple of Baha al-Din."
    (p. 187, folio 174) **The book adds that the legend is invariant except in the master's identity:
    "The legend of his birth is always identical, except that sometimes, as for example the case with the
    gaddi nashin, the Sufi Master was not Baha al-Din Zakariyya, but Shah Rukn-e Alam (d. 1335), another
    later saint of Multan." (p. 187, folio 174)**

### Doubts the notes raise on this row

- **Against `shrine_index.tsv`, recorded and not resolved.**
  - `shrine-of-makhdoom-jahaniyan-jahangasht`: the book prints **"(d. 1383)"** and calls him **"a saint
    of Multan"** (p. 189, folio 176); the archive row carries **1384** and locates the shrine at **Uch
    Sharif, Bahawalpur District**. The book also prints **"(1308–84)"** for the same man at p. 56
    (folio 43), and the id's own spelling is *jahaniyan*, one `y`, where the book prints "Jahaniyyan".
  - `shrine-of-sachal-sarmast`: the book gives 1825 and 1829; the archive row carries **1827**.
  - `dargah-pir-ratan-nath-jee`: the book puts the figure's dargah mandir in **Delhi**; the archive row
    is at **Jhanda Bazar, Peshawar**. **Three readings are open and the book settles none**: a separate
    Delhi dargah mandir of the same figure, a different figure, or a mislocated archive row.
  - `shrine-at-odero-lal-udero-lal-teerath-asthan`: the book gives "the darbar of Udero Lal, the huge
    sanctuary **close to Hyderabad**" and "Jhulelal/Shaykh Tahir" (p. 184, folio 171); the archive gives
    **Odero Lal Village (near Tando Adam), Matiari District** and "Sheikh Tahir / Udero Lal (Jhulelal)".
    **Divergent but not contradictory.**
  - `shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya` (1267) and `shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam` (1335): **the death years
    agree with the archive**; only the order's spelling differs, "Sohrawardi" for "Suhrawardi" (p. 187,
    folio 174).
  - `dargah-of-khwaja-muhammad-zaman-luari-sharif`: the archive's silsila **Naqshbandi** matches the
    Annexes passage on Lowari Sharif, **but the row's principal figure, Khwaja Muhammad Zaman, is not
    named anywhere in the book: (not stated).**

- **Two person-level mappings already made that rest on a bare name and must be weighed as such.**
  `mazar-e-iqbal` — one sentence on Iqbal's reading of Hallaj (p. 102, folio 89) and one explanatory
  endnote (p. 212, folio 199); **the row's 1938 must not be back-filled: the book gives no birth year,
  no death year and no tomb.** `sant-bhagat-kanwar-ram-temple-chak` — a parenthesis inside a note about
  somebody else, the pir of Bharchundi, where Bhagat Kanwar Ram appears only as the man whose
  assassination an ancestor was accused of ordering (p. 216, folio 203); **the printed "(1885–1939)"
  agrees with the archive's 1939 and adds a birth year, which is the one piece of new data — no place,
  no date of the assassination, no temple: (not stated).** Likewise `garh-maharaja-shorkot` on "Sultan
  Bahu's poetry, he being an important seventeenth-century Sufi from Punjab" (p. 121, folio 108), **a
  literary parallel drawn by a third party, not the book's own account — the row's 1691 and Sarwari
  Qadiri must not be back-filled**; and `shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya` on one clause in which the saint
  acts only as the initiator of Pithoro Pir (p. 178, folio 165), **no Multan, no death year, no
  shrine.**

### Place-level material: Multan

*The book's place-level material on "Multan" (8 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan")
  — **place-level; the book's four bare Multan clauses distinguish none of these eight rows.** Bedil "was
  the scion of a Qurayshi family that **migrated from Multan two generations back**" (p. 98, folio 85,
  endnote 11); "in **1397**, Pir Muhammad was in Multan" (p. 189, folio 176); "with Multan as capital"
  (p. 216, folio 203); "settled in Multan" (p. 227, folio 214). **The three Multan occurrences that carry
  a saint — Baha al-Din Zakariyya "the famous Sufi of Multan", Shah Rukn-e Alam "another later saint of
  Multan", Makhdum Jahaniyyan Jahangasht "a saint of Multan" — are in those rows' own subsections above.
  Note also that the book's "Ali Akbar" (p. 41, folio 28) is a member of Husayn's family and is NOT the
  archive's Shah Ali Akbar Shamsi, and that "the Sabzwaris" (p. 180, folio 167; index "Sabzwaris 167")
  are a Sayyid lineage of Sehwan, not the Multan saint.**

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the eighteenth century"; book's historical prose, hedged "It is possible"): on the Nanakpanth among Sindh's Hindus: "The Nanakpanth would have been initiated later. It is possible that the Aroras imported this cult from Punjab during their migrations in Sindh in the eighteenth century. The Nanakpanth is a form of Sikhism that does not recognize the authority of the Khalsa" (p. 43, folio 30).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the second half of the nineteenth century"; book's historical prose, citing Oberoi 1994): "the first attempt to rationalize and uniformize the Sikh tradition was implemented. This process of normalizing the Sikh tradition was centred on Punjab, and it did not concern the Sindhi Nanakpanthis who were heterodox Sikhs" (p. 53, folio 40).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated, historical; author's argument citing Lorenzen 1996): "The Nirguni tradition was more influential in Northern India, especially in Punjab, where the Sikh tradition historically derived from the Nirguni tradition of the Bhakti" (p. 89, folio 76).
- (book's word: "the southern present Punjab"; refers to: an unstated past time ("at the time when"); author's argument): the shared devotional culture "would be called Sindhian culture, which refers to a set of social, religious and cultural traits that existed in the Sindhi area at the time when it encompassed the southern present Punjab, and also the territories of Rajasthan and Gujarat" (p. 53, folio 40). The book does not say whether "present Punjab" means the Pakistani province. The Rajasthan/Gujarat half is under `not attached`.
- (book's word: "Southern Punjab", "South Punjab"; refers to: present day for the language; "the Middle Ages" for the historical claim; author's argument, in an endnote, the glossary and the main text): Ch. 6 n. 11: "The Siraiki language is mainly spoken in Southern Punjab, with Multan as capital. Interestingly, this area was a part of Sindh during the Middle Ages" (p. 216, folio 203). Glossary: Siraiki is "a language spoken in Northern Sindh and Southern Punjab" (p. 220, folio 207). In the main text, for some specialists the Siraiki of Northern Sindh is "a distinct language from the Siraiki of South Punjab" (p. 171, folio 158).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; author's argument, in an endnote and Annex 2): "the Chishtiyya was not widespread in Sindh, while it is dominant in Punjab and North India" (General conclusion n. 1, p. 217, folio 204). "The Chishtiyya is usually considered to be one of the most powerful Sufi orders in Northern India and Punjab" (p. 227, folio 214). The book does not say which Punjab.
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: "the Pax Britannica imposed by the colonial rule", and present-day scholarship; author's argument): scholars of Sindhi studies treat Sindh's religious encounters "as if comparable dynamics were not implemented in other regions, be they neighbouring Sindh, such as Punjab, Rajasthan or Gujarat, or elsewhere". "During the Pax Britannica imposed by the colonial rule, the exchange of goods and products was reinforced between the different regions of the north-western Indian subcontinent, including Punjab, Rajasthan, Gujarat and Sindh". Beyond the merchants there were "networks of circulating knowledge, and especially religious knowledge" (p. 196, folio not stated). The book does not give this colonial "Punjab" any boundary.

The endnote gives the source as "“Hindū hameśah se islām kā duśman rahā hai,” Urdu class V, Punjab Textbooks Board, Lahore 2002 (see Nayyar and Salim 2005, 20)" (p. 28, folio not stated, endnote 7).

## From Pnina Werbner and Helene Basu (eds.), Embodying Charisma

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/werbner_basu_embodying_charisma.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

### Province-level material: punjab

*The book's `punjab` bullets, copied into every existing entry in the province (Rauf, 8 and 10 October 2026). They speak of the province, not of this shrine.*

- (book's word: "Punjabi villages"; refers to: period not stated, ethnographic present of Werth's chapter; editors' introduction, Werbner and Basu, summarising Lukas Werth's chapter 4): "This is reflected even at the most local level, in the countless shrines to unknown saints found on the outskirts of Punjabi villages. Against the unique biographies of named saints, these anonymous shrines, Lukas Werth discloses here, call for explanation"; their anonymity is "a key feature, he suggests, of their placement as mediators between the dangerous forces of nature and cultural continuity" (p. 23, folio 12). Werth's own fieldwork is at Rajpur in the salt range (see Sweep notes); the generalisation to "Punjabi villages" is the editors'.
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; contributor's endnote, Lukas Werth, ch. 4, n. 9): "I could not find in this case expressive allusions to Khidr, who is often called Zinda pir in Punjab, because he also is thought to have disappeared alive (see Schimmel 1975: 202)" (p. 101, folio 90).
- (book's word: "Punjab"; refers to: period not stated; contributor's argument, Jamal Malik, ch. 9): "The cult of the holy man (pîr) and the associated folk piety is particularly evident in the countryside, where major landlords and large-scale land concentrations are crucial features, especially in Punjab and Sindh. In many cases, the local pîr represents the local landlord or is at least closely related to him" (p. 199, folio 188).

## From Shemeem Burney Abbas, The Female Voice in Sufi Ritual

*Integrated 12 October 2026 from `entries/book_takeaways/abbas_female_voice_sufi_ritual.md` (reviewed: no). Every line below the heading is copied verbatim from that file; nothing was added or reworded. Page references are as the takeaways give them.*

- **Saint** — **No dates and no silsila anywhere: all (not stated)**; 1267 and "Suhrawardi" appear
  nowhere. The book prints "Hazrat Bahauddin Zakariya (d. [text-layer?: numeral dropped — a death date
  is printed here and does not extract])" (p. 25, folio not stated). A son is named: he is "the father
  of Shaikh Sadruddin of Multan" (p. 193, folio not stated). The index files him under `Suﬁ Saints
  belonging to Pakistan-Indian silsilās` as `Zakariya, Hazrat Bahauddin, xix, xxiv, [locator dropped]`
  — **two printed roman locators survive** (p. 239, folio not stated).
- **Practices & Events**
  - **A named qawwal in his assemblies, at two removes.** "On authority from Firishta he mentions one
    qawwāl named Abdullah, who came to that city from Turkey (Rum) and enlivened the assemblies of
    Shaikh Bahauddin Zakariya, the father of Shaikh Sadruddin of Multan" (p. 193, folio not stated).
    **"He" is M. Wahid Mirza, whose *Life and Works of Amir Khusrau* the note cites; the ultimate
    authority named is Firishta.**
- **Significance**
  - **The author claims maternal descent from him, as family oral tradition.** "I had listened to the
    narratives of older women in the family as they talked about my maternal family's descent from
    Hazrat Bahauddin Zakariya (d.  ) and Rukunuddin" (p. 25, folio not stated). **Reported by Abbas as
    "the narratives of older women in the family", not as documented genealogy.**

### Place-level material: Multan

*The book's place-level material on "Multan" (8 rows), from its list of rows attached at place or person level, copied into every existing entry that list names (ruling (a); Rauf, 10 October 2026). It is about the place in general, not about this shrine alone.*

- **prahladpuri-temple**, **shrine-of-bahauddin-zakariya**, **shrine-of-hafiz-muhammad-jamal-multani**, **shrine-of-hazrat-shah-ali-akbar-shah-ali-akbar-shamsi**, **shrine-of-shah-rukn-e-alam**, **shrine-of-shah-shams-ud-din-sabzwari**, **shrine-of-shah-yusaf-gardez**, **shrine-of-syed-musa-pak** (the book's "Multan" — 8 rows)
  - **A shrine in Multan the author visited and the book expressly declines to identify.** "This may be
    the renowned Hazrat Shams Tabriz (d. [numeral dropped, text-layer]) who was the spiritual mentor of
    Maulana Rum (d. [numeral dropped, text-layer]), or another local Sufi by the same name. One of my
    students did take me to the shrine of one Hazrat Shams Tabrizi in Multan in [numeral dropped,
    text-layer]. Multiple shrines to the same person are not unusual. Sometimes it involves an actual
    relic, at other times simply a connection via a silsilā" (p. 193, folio not stated). **Under ruling
    (a) this is Multan's material and belongs to all eight rows; it has NOT been equated with any one
    of them, because the book itself refuses the identification.**
  - **Landowning Syeds as shrine patrons — the clearest shrine-economy passage in the book.** "Musicians
    such as Surraiya Multanikar are associated with the powerful landowning Syeds of Multan and
    Bahawalpur who claim descent from the prophet Muhammad's family. Their training is supported by the
    Syed households, and these musicians perform in the family shrines of the Syeds. This is partly
    because of the large landholdings where the affluent Syeds support the shrines of their ancestors.
    They arrange annual rituals such as the Eid and the Muharram ceremonies marking the tragedy of
    Kerbala" (pp. 168–169, folios not stated). **No shrine and no family is named: (not stated).**
  - "They additionally sing around shrines in Multan and Sahiwal to earn a living" (p. 199, folio not
    stated). **Again, no shrine named.**
  - Multan is a point in the Siraiki belt: "the Siraiki belt around the Multan, Bahawalpur, Mianwali,
    and northern Sind areas" (p. 148, folio not stated); the seat of a landowning political family,
    "my association with the landowning Syeds in Burewala, Multan. The family are politicians in the
    Pakistan Muslim League" (p. 202, folio not stated); the seat of a radio music director, "Ehsan
    Wagha, music director, Multan" (pp. 214–218, folios not stated); and a publishing centre, Bazm-e
    Saqafat and Qadimi Kutab Khana (pp. 207, 209, 222, 232, 235, folios not stated).
  - Historical mentions: "the Battle of Multan", where Prince Muhammad, Sultan Ghiasuddin Balban's son,
    was killed (p. 39, folio not stated); "The poet spent considerable time in Multan with Prince
